    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.1

    ↗
    श्रीशुक उवाच नन्द: पथि वच: शौरेर्न मृषेति विचिन्तयन् । हरिं जगाम शरणमुत्पातागमशङ्कित: ॥ १ ॥
    śrī-śuka uvāca
    [shree-shuka uvaacha]
    nandaḥ pathi vacaḥ śaurer
    [nandah pathi vachah shaurer]
    na mṛṣeti vicintayan
    [na mrisheti vichintayan]
    hariṁ jagāma śaraṇam
    [harim jagaama sharanam]
    utpātāgama-śaṅkitaḥ
    [utpaataagama-shankitah]

    Important Words
    • śaraṇam — shelter
      The technical word of Gauḍīya śaraṇāgati. Not a feeling but a deliberate act — placing oneself, with its six limbs (favorable acceptance, rejection of the unfavorable, faith that Bhagavān will protect, accepting Him as guardian, self-surrender, humility). Nanda's first move on hearing of danger is the move every devotee is asked to make.
    • harim — unto the Supreme Lord
      Hari literally means 'one who takes away' — He removes both inauspiciousness and the heart's attachment to it. The accusative form points to a personal Bhagavān one approaches, not an impersonal absolute one merges into.
    • vicintayan — thinking about (the danger to Kṛṣṇa)
      Present participle — sustained, ongoing reflection. Nanda's śaraṇāgati does not arise from panic but from sober vicāra. Gauḍīya tradition values this anukūla-cintana: thoughtful, devotional weighing of a situation that ripens naturally into surrender.
    • śaureḥ — of Vasudeva
      Genitive of Śauri (Vasudeva), Kṛṣṇa's biological father, the senior Vaiṣṇava whose foresight Nanda trusts. The verse models taking sādhu-vākya as authoritative — devotees confirm dangers for one another, and the wise listen.
    • mṛṣā — without purpose or cause
      Nanda's clinching thought: Vasudeva's words cannot be 'in vain.' A sober devotee does not dismiss another Vaiṣṇava's warning as exaggeration; he treats it as siddhānta until proven otherwise.
    Śukadeva Gosvāmī continued: My dear King, while Nanda Mahārāja was on the way home, he considered that what Vasudeva had said could not be false or useless. There must have been some danger of disturbances in Gokula. As Nanda Mahārāja thought about the danger for his beautiful son, Kṛṣṇa, he was afraid, and he took shelter at the lotus feet of the supreme controller.
    Purport

    Whenever there is danger, the pure devotee thinks of the protection and shelter of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. This is also advised in Bhagavad-gītā (9.33) : anityam asukhaṁ lokam imaṁ prāpya bhajasva mām. In this material world there is danger at every step ( padaṁ padaṁ yad vipadām ). Therefore a devotee has no other recourse than to take shelter of the Lord at every step.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Śukadeva Gosvāmī continued: My dear King, while Nanda Mahārāja was on the way home, he considered that what Vasudeva had said could not be false or useless. There must have been some danger of disturbances in Gokula. As Nanda Mahārāja thought about the danger for his beautiful son, Kṛṣṇa, he was afraid, and he took shelter at the lotus feet of the supreme controller.
    Commentary

    In the sixth chapter Putana assumes a beautiful form and appears in Vraja. Upon dying, she assumes her raksasi form. She attains liberation by Krsna’s mercy. Her body is burned and Nanda approaches.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    He surrendered in his mind to Hari, he who destroys all fears, since Viṣṇu was his family deity, for the auspiciousness of his son. Or, because the information could not be false, on the way back he especially thought of his son, most attractive (harim). Thinking, “I do not know what news is there” he returned to his house (śaraṇam).

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    On the road, thinking, he surrendered in his mind to Hari, he who destroys all fears. O Lord! Please protect my son, he said in pain. Or he prayed in his heart, since he was affectionate to Kṛṣṇa at all times, constantly thinking of him. Though he was always absorbed in Kṛṣṇa, now particularly he surrendered to the Lord because of the danger, since he was the best of devotees. The devotees will surrender to the Lord and not do anything else to destroy dangers. Actually he did so out of great affection for Kṛṣṇa. Or on the path he thought of his son, who was attractive (harim) because Vasudeva’s words could not be false. “I do not know what has happened there.” Thus he went to his house (śaraṇam).

    Personal Guidance
    When danger looms, the pure devotee — like Nanda — does not scheme; he takes śaraṇa at Kṛṣṇa's lotus feet at the very first sign of trouble.
    Practical Applications
    • At the first flicker of fear today — a worry about a child, a job, a diagnosis — pause and audibly take shelter of Kṛṣṇa before doing anything else.
    • Treat a trusted devotee's warning the way Nanda treated Vasudeva's words: do not dismiss it as exaggeration, act on it.
    • Recite padaṁ padaṁ yad vipadām once today and name the specific 'step' in your life where you have been pretending there is no danger.
    Analogy

    Nanda Mahārāja is the prudent merchant who hears a storm warning while still on the road home — he does not argue with the messenger, he does not gallop, he simply tightens his grip on the rope of śaraṇāgati and walks toward Kṛṣṇa's lotus feet. Like a child who hears thunder and quietly takes hold of his father's finger, the devotee at the first hint of danger reaches not for a plan but for Hari.

    Stories
    Gajendra at the lake

    Caught in the crocodile's jaws and seeing his own strength fail after a thousand divine years of struggle, the elephant-king Gajendra at last let go of self-effort and cried out to the Supreme Lord. He did not invent a strategy; he took śaraṇa.

    Kṛṣṇa as Viṣṇu appeared at once on Garuḍa, severed the crocodile, and lifted His devotee out. Nanda's reflex in 10.6.1 is the same reflex: at the first whiff of utpāta, the devotee runs not toward defense but toward Hari's feet — and that is the only running that ever saves anyone.

    Kuntī's prayer at every danger

    Queen Kuntī (SB 1.8) confesses that she actually welcomes calamity, because every danger drove her to remember Kṛṣṇa. Poison, fire, snakes, exile, the assembly of Kurus, Aśvatthāmā's brahmāstra — at each step she did not first scheme, she first remembered.

    Nanda walking home, weighing Vasudeva's words and turning his heart toward Hari, is Kuntī's instinct in male, paternal form: śaraṇa first, action after.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.2

    ↗
    कंसेन प्रहिता घोरा पूतना बालघातिनी । शिशूंश्चचार निघ्नन्ती पुरग्रामव्रजादिषु ॥ २ ॥
    kaṁsena prahitā ghorā
    [kamsena prahitaa ghoraa]

    [pootanaa baala-ghaatinee]

    Important Words
    • kaṁsena — by King Kaṁsa
      Instrumental. The danger is enacted by Pūtanā but caused by Kaṁsa — the verse traces back from the visible threat to its political source. Gauḍīya reading: the asura-mind in power deploys agents that look private; the devotee learns to see the king behind every Pūtanā.
    • ghorā — very fierce
      The same adjective Bhāgavata applies to elemental terrors. The verse refuses to let her later cosmetic beauty (verse 5) soften the underlying truth: she is ghorā at the core.
    • bāla - ghātinī — a Rākṣasī who killed (small babies)
      Compound — bāla (child) + ghātin (slayer) in feminine. The Rākṣasī's specialty is killing the small and the innocent: in Gauḍīya pastoral reading, anything that destroys the bhakti-latā in its tender, infant stage is a Pūtanā-form.
    • pura - grāma - vraja - ādiṣu — in towns, cities and villages
      Locative plural compound. The threat is not confined to the wilderness; it walks the populated paths. Māyā stalks where most people are, dressed in their own clothes.
    • nighnantī — killing
      Present participle — continuous action, not a one-off. While Nanda is on the road and Gokula is celebrating Kṛṣṇa's birth, this killing is happening right now in the next village. The devotee never assumes the war is paused.
    While Nanda Mahārāja was returning to Gokula, the same fierce Pūtanā whom Kaṁsa had previously engaged to kill babies was wandering about in the towns, cities and villages, doing her nefarious duty.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    While Nanda Mahārāja was returning to Gokula, the same fierce Pūtanā whom Kaṁsa had previously engaged to kill babies was wandering about in the towns, cities and villages, doing her nefarious duty.
    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    This verse tells the news of Vraja. Though previously his ministers persuaded him to afflict the sages, fearful Kaṁsa agreed that Pūtanā should go to kill the infants as a simultaneous method of safely before harassing the sages. She was first (pra) engaged because she was ferocious in killing infants. She killed them completely, by giving them her poisoned breast. They died immediately. Viṣṇu Purāṇa says: yasmai yasmai stanaṁ rātrau pūtanā saṁprayacchati tasya taysa kṣaṇenāṅgam bālakasyopahanyate In a moment Pūtanā killed any child to whom she offered her breast. Another version has pura-gramākarādiṣu: among the towns, villages and mining settlements. Ādiśu means “among the flocks.”

      His Holiness Sridhar Swami· 1998-10-31· English
    Personal Guidance
    Even as Kṛṣṇa enters Gokula, Kaṁsa's Pūtanā is already at work — māyā never sleeps; the devotee must stay alert in śraddhā and vairāgya.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice one 'Pūtanā' in your day — a sweet-seeming influence (a feed, a flatterer, a habit) that quietly poisons bhakti — and refuse its milk today.
    • Before stepping out, take shelter of Kṛṣṇa's holy name once with full attention, knowing that hostile forces are already in motion in the towns and streets you will enter.
    • Guard the small and vulnerable in your care — children, juniors, your own beginner's faith — by screening what you allow near them, the way Yaśodā would screen a stranger at the door.
    Analogy

    Kaṁsa's order keeps moving even after Kṛṣṇa has been carried beyond his reach — like a poisoned arrow loosed at one target that travels on, hunting any child it can find. Pūtanā is that arrow in human shape: māyā in motion through the towns while you are dreaming Gokula is safe.

    Stories
    Hiraṇyakaśipu's standing decree

    Even when Prahlāda's father slept, his daityas roamed the worlds tormenting Vaiṣṇavas under standing orders (SB 7.5). The danger to a child of the Lord was not paused by the king's mood — it was institutional, ambient, always already at work.

    Kaṁsa's Pūtanā is the same shape of evil: dispatched once, still walking. The devotee household therefore lives in constant invocation of Hari's name, not because it is paranoid, but because it is awake.

    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.3

    ↗
    न यत्र श्रवणादीनि रक्षोघ्नानि स्वकर्मसु । कुर्वन्ति सात्वतां भर्तुर्यातुधान्यश्च तत्र हि ॥ ३ ॥
    na yatra śravaṇādīni
    [na yatra shravanaadeeni]
    rakṣo-ghnāni sva-karmasu
    [raksho-ghnaani sva-karmasu]
    kurvanti sātvatāṁ bhartur
    [kurvanti saatvataam bhartur]
    yātudhānyaś ca tatra hi
    [yaatudhaanyash cha tatra hi]
    Word-for-word
    na — not yatra — wherever śravaṇa - ādīni — the activities of bhakti-yoga, beginning with hearing and chanting rakṣaḥ - ghnāni — the sound vibration to kill all danger and bad elements sva - karmasu — if one is engaged in his own occupational duty kurvanti — such things are done sātvatām bhartuḥ — of the protector of the devotees yātudhānyaḥ — disturbing elements, bad elements ca — also tatra hi — there must be .

    Important Words
    • śravaṇa - ādīni — the activities (of bhakti-yoga) beginning with hearing
      Compound — śravaṇa (hearing) + ādi (beginning with). Code for the nine limbs of bhakti enumerated by Prahlāda (SB 7.5.23): śravaṇaṁ kīrtanaṁ viṣṇoḥ, etc. The Bhāgavata's whole sādhana is compressed in this one word and placed inside sva-karma — bhakti is not an alternative to duty, it is what makes duty safe.
    • rakṣaḥ - ghnāni — destroyers of demoniac forces
      Adjectival compound modifying śravaṇa-ādīni: 'demon-killing.' The acoustic vibration of Kṛṣṇa's names and pastimes is itself the weapon. Gauḍīya doctrine: nāma is non-different from nāmī — chanting Kṛṣṇa's name is Kṛṣṇa Himself acting.
    • sva - karmasu — in one's own occupational duties
      Locative plural. The Gītā's sva-dharma carried into the Bhāgavatam: bhakti is not built by abandoning one's prescribed work but by saturating it with hearing and chanting. The kitchen, the field, the office becomes the sādhanā-kṣetra.
    • sātvatām bhartuḥ — of the protector of the devotees
      Genitive of sātvatām bhartṛ — Bhagavān as the husband-protector of the Sātvatas (devotees). A specifically Vaiṣṇava epithet; Kṛṣṇa is not a distant ruler but the bhartā of His own community, personally invested in their safety.
    • yātudhānyaḥ — disturbing elements, bad elements
      Nominative plural — the very class to which Pūtanā belongs. The verse names them only to dismiss them: where bhakti's sound is, they have no foothold.
    My dear King, wherever people in any position perform their occupational duties of devotional service by chanting and hearing [śravaṇaṁ kīrtanaṁ viṣṇoḥ], there cannot be any danger from bad elements. Therefore there was no need for anxiety about Gokula while the Supreme Personality of Godhead was personally present.
    Purport

    Śukadeva Gosvāmī spoke this verse to mitigate the anxiety of Mahārāja Parīkṣit. Mahārāja Parīkṣit was a devotee of Kṛṣṇa, and therefore when he understood that Pūtanā was causing disturbances in Gokula, he was somewhat perturbed. Śukadeva Gosvāmī therefore assured him that there was no danger in Gokula. Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura has sung: nāmāśraya kari’ yatane tumi, thākaha āpana kāje. Everyone is thus advised to seek shelter in the chanting of the Hare Kṛṣṇa mahā-mantra and remain engaged in his own occupational duty. There is no loss in this, and the gain is tremendous. Even from a material point of view, everyone should take to chanting the Hare Kṛṣṇa mantra to be saved from all kinds of danger. This world is full of danger ( padaṁ padaṁ yad vipadām ). Therefore we should be encouraged to chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa mahā-mantra so that in our family, society, neighborhood and nation, everything will be smooth and free from danger.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    My dear King, wherever people in any position perform their occupational duties of devotional service by chanting and hearing [śravaṇaṁ kīrtanaṁ viṣṇoḥ SB 7.5.23], there cannot be any danger from bad elements. Therefore there was no need for anxiety about Gokula while the Supreme Personality of Godhead was personally present.
    Commentary

    This verse is spoken to allay the fears of Maharaja Pariksit for Krsna’s safety. In those places where people engage in scriptural duties like sacrifices without devotional acts such as hearing stories of the Lord, who is the life of the devotees, the raksasis become powerful. Then what to speak of places where these devotional acts are the primary means? There the raksasis can have no influence. And what to speak of the place where the Lord is personally present?

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    It was said that Pūtanā killed children in the towns and villages. What happened to Nanda’s boys? This verse answers. Where there is no hearing during sacrifices and other duties (sva-karmasu) mainly for attaining one’s material goals, what to speak of no having hearing as the main item, the demons will afflict. Hearing about the devotees (sātvatām) destroys the demons, what to speak of hearing about the Lord (bhartuḥ). The word ca means tu (but) or can mean other wicked persons. Certainly (hi) such persons will be strong (these words should be supplied) in those places. Or it is an answer to the question “Did she kill all the babies?” The answer is the same. Others who were inimical to the Lord were killed by him. Thus it is shown that Kaṁsa was foolish. This was all arranged by the Lord. The coming of the demons was for the purpose of increasing the pastimes of the Lord, which produce bliss for all people. These disturbances arranged by the Lord were produced by the Lord’s līlā-śakti according to the Lord’s rasas, in order to increase Yaśodā’s prema for Kṛṣṇa. The līlā-śakti is composed of Śrī, Bhū and Līlā. These three śaktis of Vaikuṇṭha are well known in the Padma Purāṇa. This śakti is described as Tulasī and Vṛndā in the Kārtika-māhātmya of Padma Purāṇa. She is called Vṛndā in Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes and lives in Vṛndāvana.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    It was said that Pūtanā killed children in the towns and villages. What happened to Nanda’s boys? This verse answers. Where there is no hearing during sacrifices and other duties (sva-karmasu) mainly for attaining one’s material goals, what to speak of not having hearing as the main item, the demons cause affliction. Hearing about the devotees (sātvatām) destroys the demons, what to speak of hearing about the Lord (bhartuḥ). The word ca means tu (but) or can mean other wicked persons. Certainly (hi) such persons will be strong (these words should be supplied) in those places. Or it is an answer to the question “Did she kill all the babies?” The answer is the same. All the children were safe who lived where there was hearing about the Lord. Because of the spread of bhakti everywhere with the appearance of the Lord, at that time the children could not be killed. Or where there is hearing about the Lord the demons do not exist. Since the Lord lived personally in Gokula, there was always glorification of the Lord and all auspiciousness. This is explained in verse 7.

    Related values & verses

    Related lectures
    • BRasamritaSw Hindi SB 10-06-03 - Bhagvan Avashya Raksha Karte Hai - 2013-10-01 ISKCON Mira Road
      His Holiness Bhakti Rasamrita Swami· 2013-10-01· Hindi
    Personal Guidance
    Where śravaṇaṁ-kīrtanaṁ of Kṛṣṇa goes on within sva-karma, no rākṣasic force can take hold — chant, and stay at your post.
    Practical Applications
    • Before starting today's work, chant one round of the Hare Kṛṣṇa mahā-mantra and explicitly offer the day's duties as devotional service.
    • Notice the next moment of anxiety about your family or nation, and refuse to manage it mentally — instead, audibly chant Kṛṣṇa's names right then.
    • Do not abandon your sva-karma to chase 'spiritual' settings; perform today's ordinary occupational duty with śravaṇaṁ-kīrtanaṁ running alongside it.
    Analogy

    Where śravaṇa-kīrtana goes on inside one's prescribed work, the home becomes like a sandalwood grove that snakes cannot enter — not because the snakes have been chased away, but because the very fragrance of the place repels them. Kṛṣṇa's holy name is that fragrance; sva-karma performed in His remembrance is the grove that grows up around the soul.

    Stories
    Ambarīṣa Mahārāja and Durvāsā

    King Ambarīṣa (SB 9.4–5) ruled an empire, performed Ekādaśī vrata, and engaged each sense in Kṛṣṇa's service — eyes seeing the Deity, ears hearing His glories, hands cleaning His temple. When the fierce Durvāsā Muni unleashed a kṛtyā-fire against him, the Lord's own Sudarśana cakra appeared and protected him on the spot.

    Ambarīṣa was at his post — śravaṇa-kīrtana running inside sva-karma — so the Lord's protection was not a request, it was already there. This is the verse's promise in living form.

    Prahlāda untouched in the assembly

    Bound, thrown from cliffs, fed poison, set on fire, trampled by elephants — through every torture Prahlāda Mahārāja (SB 7.5) simply kept hearing and chanting about Viṣṇu. No yātudhāna got through, because Hari was personally present where śravaṇa-kīrtana never stopped.

    The verse asks the householder to read his own kitchen and office the same way: my post is the place where the Name is being kept alive.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.4

    ↗
    सा खेचर्येकदोत्पत्य पूतना नन्दगोकुलम् । योषित्वा माययात्मानं प्राविशत् कामचारिणी ॥ ४ ॥

    Important Words
    • kāma - cāriṇī — one who could move according to her own desire
      Compound — kāma (desire) + cāriṇī (moving, feminine). The mystic potency of going wherever one pleases. In Gauḍīya reading, the unhinged 'freedom to do as I like' that the asura-line cultivates is exactly the opposite of the devotee's discipline of going wherever Kṛṣṇa wills.
    • māyayā — by mystic power
      Instrumental of māyā. Here māyā means deceptive yoga-siddhi, not Yoga-māyā (Kṛṣṇa's internal potency). The verse contrasts the two: same word, opposite ends — Yoga-māyā veils Kṛṣṇa to deepen love, mahā-māyā veils a demon to deceive the loving.
    • khe - carī — one who traveled in outer space
      Compound — kha (sky) + cara (mover). Sky-going siddhi sounds spiritual; the verse cautions that mystic mobility without bhakti is fully compatible with bāla-ghātinī intent.
    • yoṣitvā — converting into a very beautiful woman
      Gerund. Pūtanā does not enter as herself; she translates herself into the form most likely to be welcomed. Māyā always reshapes itself to fit the household it intends to corrupt.
    • nanda - gokulam — Nanda Mahārāja's Gokula
      Compound — Nanda's pasture-village, the most sanctified spot on the surface of the earth. The Rākṣasī's audacity is the measure of the danger: even a place where Kṛṣṇa is personally present is approached when the disguise is good enough.
    Once upon a time, Pūtanā Rākṣasī, who could move according to her desire and was wandering in outer space, converted herself by mystic power into a very beautiful woman and thus entered Gokula, the abode of Nanda Mahārāja.
    Purport

    Rākṣasīs learn mystic powers by which they can travel in outer space without machines. In some parts of India there are still such mystical witches, who can sit on a stick and use it to fly from one place to another in a very short time. This art was known to Pūtanā. Assuming the feature of a very beautiful woman, she entered Nanda Mahārāja’s abode, Gokula.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Once upon a time, Pūtanā Rākṣasī, who could move according to her desire and was wandering in outer space, converted herself by mystic power into a very beautiful woman and thus entered Gokula, the abode of Nanda Mahārāja.
    Commentary

    In order that the pastime of killing Putana be performed, by the inspiration of the lila sakti of the Lord, Putana entered Gokula as if invited by death to be killed. With this intention the verse is spoken. One day in the evening, flying from the sky (utpatya), she entered Gokula. An alternate reading is upetya (arriving). By illusion she took on the form of a beautiful women (yosita). All were so much enamored with her beauty that she had access to all the houses and towns. Though the Lord’s illusory energy has no desire to bewilder the eternal devotees of Vraja, in order execute the pastimes of Krsna, Putana was able to exert her illusions, like a magician, by the will of the Lord.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    She went at night, according to Parāśara and Vaiśampāyaṇa, flying in the sky. Because she was of ferocious demeanor she transformed herself into a woman. The verb form should actually be yoṣāyitvā. It is abbreviated by poetic license.

    Related values & verses
    Related values
    Discriminative WisdomAnahankarah (Freedom from False Ego)Abhaya (Fearlessness)Vinaya (Humility / Modesty)

    • Padaseva Pr SB 10-06-04 Oriya
      His Grace Padaseva Prabhu· Oriya
    • PSarasvati Sw SB 10-06-04 Hindi - 2020-01-01 Punjabi Bagh
      His Holiness Prabodhananda Sarasvati Swami· 2020-01-01· Hindi
    • Sridhar Sw SB 10-06-04 - Our business is preaching - Ljubljana 2001-04-20
      His Holiness Sridhar Swami· 2001-04-20· English
    Personal Guidance
    Māyā does not knock as a demon — she enters Gokula disguised as beauty; train discriminative sight to recognize Pūtanā in pleasing forms.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice one 'beautiful' offer today — a flattering message, an alluring purchase, an easy shortcut — and ask whose interest it actually serves.
    • Before admitting any new influence (media, company, content) into your inner Gokula, pause and test it against Kṛṣṇa-consciousness rather than its surface charm.
    • Refuse to judge people, content, or invitations by their pleasing exterior; cultivate vairāgya by looking past form to intention and effect.
    Analogy

    Pūtanā arriving in Gokula as a beautiful woman is like a viper crawling into a flower-shop and curling into the petals of a rose — the buyers, charmed by color, never bend close enough to see the venom. Yoga-māyā has already taught the Vrajavāsīs to love beauty in Kṛṣṇa; mahā-māyā exploits the same eye when it is not anchored in Him.

    Stories
    Mārīca as the golden deer

    When Rāvaṇa needed to lure Rāma away from Sītā, he sent the demon Mārīca disguised as a deer of impossible loveliness (Rāmāyaṇa, Araṇya-kāṇḍa). Sītā, drawn by the beauty, asked for it; Rāma, knowing better, still went after it out of love for Her — and Rāvaṇa walked into the empty hut.

    Demons rarely come ugly. The verse asks the devotee to distrust beauty that has not first been verified by its relation to Bhagavān — kāma-cāriṇī forms, beautiful and free-moving, are precisely how the asura-line operates.

    Important Words
    • valgu - smita - apāṅga - visarga - vīkṣitaiḥ — by her casting her smiling sidelong glances
      Long compound: valgu (charming) + smita (smile) + apāṅga (sidelong) + visarga (release) + vīkṣita (glance). The grammar piles up the seductive details to show how the senses are taken by stages. Gauḍīya note: the gopīs' real apāṅga-vīkṣita is for Kṛṣṇa alone; the counterfeit copies the form but not the object.
    • śriyam — the goddess of fortune
      Accusative of Śrī (Lakṣmī). The gopīs' kindly misreading: they see Pūtanā and assume Lakṣmī has come to see her Lord. In Gauḍīya rasa-vicāra, only Kṛṣṇa's hlādinī-śakti (Rādhā and the gopīs themselves) holds the topmost place in Vraja; the Vaikuṇṭha-Lakṣmī cannot enter Vraja's mādhurya — yet māyā can mimic Her form.
    • patim — her husband
      Accusative — Kṛṣṇa as Lakṣmī's Lord, the husband of Śrī (Nārāyaṇa). The gopīs' assumption is theologically literate, which is precisely what makes them slow to suspect: a wife coming to see her divine husband is the most innocent possible reading.
    • ambhoja — (holding) a lotus flower
      The traditional iconographic mark of Lakṣmī. Pūtanā has not actually shown a lotus; the gopīs project it because the rest of the costume fits. Māyā works by completion — give the eye enough cues and it will draw the missing one in.
    • vraja - okasām — of the inhabitants of Gokula
      Compound — vraja (pasture-land) + okas (dweller). The Vrajavāsīs, the highest devotees in Gauḍīya rasa-tattva, are still capable of being momentarily charmed by a counterfeit Lakṣmī. The verse is not a critique of them; it is a warning to us: if even the Vrajavāsīs needed Kṛṣṇa's intervention, we certainly do.
    Her hips were full, her breasts were large and firm, seeming to overburden her slim waist, and she was dressed very nicely. Her hair, adorned with a garland of mallikā flowers, was scattered about her beautiful face. Her earrings were brilliant, and as she smiled very attractively, glancing upon everyone, her beauty drew the attention of all the inhabitants of Vraja, especially the men. When the gopīs saw her, they thought that the beautiful goddess of fortune, holding a lotus flower in her hand, had come to see her husband, Kṛṣṇa.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Her hips were full, her breasts were large and firm, seeming to overburden her slim waist, and she was dressed very nicely. Her hair, adorned with a garland of mallikā flowers, was scattered about her beautiful face. Her earrings were brilliant, and as she smiled very attractively, glancing upon everyone, her beauty drew the attention of all the inhabitants of Vraja, especially the men. When the gopīs saw her, they thought that the beautiful goddess of fortune, holding a lotus flower in her hand, had come to see her husband, Kṛṣṇa.
    Commentary

    Seeing that beautiful woman with marks of lotus on her hands, the gopis concluded that she was the embodiment of all wealth and jewels. She must have come to see her husband, Narayana, the istadeva of Nanda Maharaja. Putana had broad waist and breasts contrasted to her thin waist. She was attractive (vanita). She was so attractive that the Vrajavasis let her enter the inner chambers of their houses without obstruction.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Jasmines bound together were tied in her hair, making her very beautiful. He face was decorated with locks of hair shining with the effulgence of her swinging earrings. She was acting attractively (manoharantīm). The gopīs thought she was the wealth of the universe (śriyam), since she held lotuses in her hands. Why would she come? She seemed to be looking for her husband, or to bless someone pious person who had taken shelter of her. That person was Kṛṣṇa alone. Thus she could not be stopped from approaching him. Pati means a husband but Śrīdhara Svāmī does not accept that meaning since later she is described as having motherly affection. Her waist was thin as if being squeezed between her wide hips and her huge breasts. The word kṛcchra (pain) is used instead of kṛśa(thin) since the cause (pain) and effect (thinness) are equated. Because she was so attractive, the gopīs became most attracted. There is no redundancy in the description (saying manoharaṇīm vanitām and sriyam) since the first phrase applies to Pūtanā and the second applies to the gopīs’ perception of her beauty.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Jasmines bound together were tied in her hair, making her very beautiful. He face was decorated with locks of hair shining with the effulgence of her swinging earrings. She was acting attractively (manoharantīm). The gopīs thought she was the wealth of the universe (śriyam), since she held lotuses in her hands. She held play lotuses in her hands to produce great beauty. Why would she come? She seemed to be looking for her husband. The word iva (as if) indicates it was not actually so. Or like Lakṣmī she had come to see the protector (patim) of the gopīs, since Kṛṣṇa was the son of Nanda. Or the gopīs thought that the woman was like Lakṣmī coming from her planet to see Nārāyaṇa. This appearance was possible because of her astonishing beauty and skillful dress.

    Personal Guidance
    Pūtanā arrives dressed as Lakṣmī — māyā's most dangerous form is the one that mimics auspiciousness; only Kṛṣṇa-conscious discrimination sees through it.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice today one 'attractive' offering — a person, a feed, a deal — and ask whether it carries Kṛṣṇa within it or only borrows the look of śrī.
    • Refuse to judge people or proposals by surface beauty alone; pause and check the intent behind the smile before you let it into your home or heart.
    • Before opening your phone or door, chant Kṛṣṇa's name once and ask Him to give you the gopīs' eyes — quick to see Him, slow to be charmed by what merely resembles Him.
    Analogy

    Pūtanā walking into Vraja in mallikā garland and earrings is like counterfeit currency printed so well that even the priests stop to admire the seal. The gopīs mistake her for Lakṣmī coming to see her husband — the lie is so close to the truth that only Kṛṣṇa's own glance can finally tell them apart.

    Stories
    Śūrpaṇakhā in the āśrama

    When Śūrpaṇakhā approached Lord Rāma in the Daṇḍaka forest (Rāmāyaṇa, Araṇya-kāṇḍa), she had assumed a form of disarming loveliness. Only when her actual intent rose — possessive lust, then murderous fury at being refused — did her true nature break through the disguise.

    The Vraja gopīs in 10.6.5–6 stand at exactly that earlier moment: the surface is still intact, the smile is still working, and only the Lord's own protective potency will let the disguise come apart in time.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.7

    ↗
    बालग्रहस्तत्र विचिन्वती शिशून् यद‍ृच्छया नन्दगृहेऽसदन्तकम् । बालं प्रतिच्छन्ननिजोरुतेजसं ददर्श तल्पेऽग्निमिवाहितं भसि ॥ ७ ॥
    bāla-grahas tatra vicinvatī śiśūn
    [baala-grahas tatra vichinvatee shishoon]
    yadṛcchayā nanda-gṛhe ’sad-antakam
    [yadrichchayaa nanda-grihe ’sad-antakam]
    bālaṁ praticchanna-nijoru-tejasaṁ
    [baalam pratichchanna-nijoru-tejasam]
    dadarśa talpe ’gnim ivāhitaṁ bhasi
    [dadarsha talpe ’gnim ivaahitam bhasi]

    Important Words
    • praticchanna — covered
      Past participle — His effulgence is deliberately concealed, not absent. Gauḍīya doctrine: Kṛṣṇa's avatāra-līlā depends on selective veiling by Yoga-māyā so that human-style affection (vātsalya, sakhya, mādhurya) can develop. If His full aiśvarya were visible, no mother would dare embrace Him.
    • nija - uru - tejasam — whose unlimited power (was covered)
      Compound — nija (own) + uru (great) + tejas (power, brilliance). The tejas is His, not lent; the verse insists that infant Kṛṣṇa is svayaṁ-bhagavān in full, not a graduated incarnation that 'becomes' divine later.
    • asat - antakam — destroyer of the demons (asat)
      Compound — asat (the demoniac, the false) + antaka (ender). One of the avatāra-purposes named in the Gītā (paritrāṇāya sādhūnāṁ, vināśāya ca duṣkṛtām). Pūtanā is unknowingly bringing herself to the very form whose business is to end the asat-line.
    • bāla - grahaḥ — the witch whose business was to kill small babies
      Compound — bāla (child) + graha (seizer, malefic planet). Bhāgavata uses graha-vocabulary because in Vedic culture certain demoniac forces 'seize' children the way malefic grahas afflict horoscopes. Verses 27–29 will name many such grahas — Pūtanā is their archetype.
    • yadṛcchayā — independently / by (the Lord's) will
      Ablative-instrumental of yadṛcchā — 'as it happened' on the surface, but the purport reads it as the higher arrangement of Kṛṣṇa's potency. Nothing 'just happens' in Vraja-līlā; even Pūtanā's free movement is the rope Yoga-māyā pays out to bring her into Kṛṣṇa's reach.
    While searching for small children, Pūtanā, whose business was to kill them, entered the house of Nanda Mahārāja unobstructed, having been sent by the superior potency of the Lord. Without asking anyone’s permission, she entered Nanda Mahārāja’s room, where she saw the child sleeping in bed, His unlimited power covered like a powerful fire covered by ashes. She could understand that this child was not ordinary, but was meant to kill all demons.
    Purport

    Demons are always busy creating disturbances and killing. But the child lying on the bed in the house of Nanda Mahārāja was meant to kill many demons.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    While searching for small children, Pūtanā, whose business was to kill them, entered the house of Nanda Mahārāja unobstructed, having been sent by the superior potency of the Lord. Without asking anyone's permission, she entered Nanda Mahārāja's room, where she saw the child sleeping in bed, His unlimited power covered like a powerful fire covered by ashes. She could understand that this child was not ordinary, but was meant to kill all demons.
    Commentary

    Putana, the killer of children (bala graha -baby catcher) saw the child (balam) who, though the killer of the wicked (asad antakam), appeared easy to kill. At that time he appeared like a fire covered by ash, hiding his powers.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    This verse continues the description from verse 4. The word bāla-grahaḥ is not in the feminine since it belongs to a class of words which retain their original gender when used as an adjective (even if the person is female.) She searched out babies in Gokula, without having to make effort (yadṛcchayā), since she was inspired by the līlā-śakti. Sudden coming to Nanda’s house while searching, she saw Kṛṣṇa on the bed. Yadṛcchayā means independently according to Amara-koṣa. Thought the child was the death to demons, he manifested his sweetness at this time. She saw him on the bed, while he hid his powers in front of her. An example is given. He was like a fire covered by ashes. That she attracted all the people of Vraja is the action of the līlā-śakti.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Searching for children in Gokula, she saw the child, who was performing childhood pastimes (bālam), who puts an end to demons, and who was sleeping (āhitam) on a bed. This also excludes Balarāma as the child described, since he sometimes overlooks the demons. Balarāma was in Rohiṇī’s house. Bālam indicates that Kṛṣṇa was performing bāla pastimes as his nature or it expresses Śukadeva’s special bhakti. Kṛṣṇa caused the great power of Pūtanā to be destroyed (praticchanna-nijoru-tejasamur). This indicates his great power. An example is given. It was like fire hidden in charcoal. The charcoal can manifest the intense burning of fire. Did she not become fearful? Why was such an evil person able to see the Lord? It was because of the manifestation of the Lord’s powers (yadṛcchayā). This reason is also apparent later. She also dressed up attractively as a cowherd women so the women thought she was Lakṣmī. Thus they did not prevent her from approaching.

    Personal Guidance
    Kṛṣṇa lay in Nanda's bed with His tejas veiled like fire under ashes — the Lord enters our ordinary life cloaked, ready to slay every Pūtanā that comes for the soul.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice today where Kṛṣṇa is acting quietly in your life under the 'ashes' of ordinary circumstance, and thank Him by name for that hidden protection.
    • Refuse the demonic strategy of Pūtanā — masquerading affection that feeds on the innocent — and audit one influence (a feed, a friendship, a habit) that wears a mother's face but poisons your bhakti.
    • Speak Kṛṣṇa's holy name aloud before sleep tonight, entrusting your household and dependents to the same Lord who let no demon touch the cradle in Gokula.
    Analogy

    Baby Kṛṣṇa lying on the bed with His tejas veiled is fire banked under a heap of ashes in a village kitchen — to the casual eye, only a soft pile; to the one who lifts the ashes, an inferno that will burn the hand off the demon. Pūtanā walks straight up to the fireplace thinking it has gone out.

    Stories
    Vāmana before Bali

    When Lord Vāmana approached King Bali's sacrifice (SB 8.18–22), He came as a small brahmacārī boy carrying a water pot. Śukrācārya saw through the disguise, but Bali, generous and devotional, gave his promise anyway. Only when Vāmana began to step did the tejas unveil — three worlds covered in two strides.

    The Pūtanā encounter is the reverse motion of the same līlā: a demon walks confidently up to the 'small boy' on the bed, having no idea that the same banked fire of Vāmana lies under the ashes of infancy.

    Important Words
    • anantam — the Unlimited
      Accusative of ananta — He who has no end. The verse pointedly uses this name as Pūtanā places Him on her lap: she thinks she is handling something small and finite; she is in fact handling the limitless. Acintya-bhedābheda corollary: the Unlimited can voluntarily appear as a finite infant without ceasing to be Unlimited.
    • antakam — for her own annihilation
      Accusative — 'the ender.' Sound-near to anantam by deliberate pairing in the verse: the Ananta is her antaka. The play of letters carries the doctrine.
    • rajju - dhīḥ — one who thinks (a snake to be) a rope
      Compound — rajju (rope) + dhī (intelligence). The classical Vedānta example of mis-cognition appears here inverted: the Advaitin example treats the rope as truth and the snake as illusion; here the snake is the truth and 'rope' is the deluded reading. Gauḍīya commentators love this inversion — it warns against any philosophy that calls the dangerous thing harmless because it has decided in advance that all is one.
    • cara - acara - ātmā — the Supersoul of all moving and non-moving beings
      Compound — cara (moving) + acara (non-moving) + ātmā (Self). Even as the apparent infant, Kṛṣṇa is the Paramātmā of every entity, including Pūtanā herself. The very life she will lose is His to take, because it was His to give.
    • nimīlita - īkṣaṇaḥ — with His eyes closed
      Compound — nimīlita (closed) + īkṣaṇa (eye). The līlā-touch: Kṛṣṇa pretends fear, closing His eyes, to let the demon think the plan is working. Vaiṣṇava ācāryas read this as ānanda-līlā — the Lord enacts a kind of stage-acting so that the destruction may unfold as pastime, not bare execution.
    Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa, the all-pervading Supersoul, lying on the bed, understood that Pūtanā, a witch who was expert in killing small children, had come to kill Him. Therefore, as if afraid of her, Kṛṣṇa closed His eyes. Thus Pūtanā took upon her lap Him who was to be her own annihilation, just as an unintelligent person places a sleeping snake on his lap, thinking the snake to be a rope.
    Purport

    In this verse there are two perplexities. When Kṛṣṇa saw that Pūtanā had come to kill Him, He thought that since this woman was present with motherly affection, although artificial, He had to offer her a benediction. Therefore He looked at her with a little perplexity and then closed His eyes again. Pūtanā Rākṣasī also was perplexed. She was not intelligent enough to understand that she was taking a sleeping snake on her lap; she thought the snake to be an ordinary rope. The two words antakam and anantam are contradictory. Because of not being intelligent, Pūtanā thought that she could kill her antakam, the source of her annihilation; but because He is ananta, unlimited, no one can kill Him.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Lord Śrī Kṛṣṇa, the all-pervading Supersoul, lying on the bed, understood that Pūtanā, a witch who was expert in killing small children, had come to kill Him. Therefore, as if afraid of her, Kṛṣṇa closed His eyes. Thus Pūtanā took upon her lap Him who was to be her own annihilation, just as an unintelligent person places a sleeping snake on his lap, thinking the snake to be a rope.
    Commentary

    Here is shown an opportunity for the Lord’s power of omniscience (sarvajnata sakti) to render service at the time of approaching evil. Though Krsna could understand Purana’s identity, he lay there with his eyes closed like a normal child. Why did he close his eyes? To show his fearfulness as a small child, to avoid seeing such an inauspicious person, to avoid making the violence become auspicious through the touch of his auspicious glance, to avoid shame of killing a woman acting as a mother, and to avoid seeing the inauspiciousness of Putana’s death. Then Putana placed him in her lap. He is called ananta, without end, and antakam, he who brings about the end. This was an opportunity for his destructive energy (samhara sakti) to operate. That person who is without limit in all space and time, Putana held on her lap. Antakam and ananta are also contradictory words to express this wonder, adbhuta rasa. As a foolish person holds a sleeping snake, thinking it is a rope, so Putana foolishly held Krsna, thinking him an ordinary, helpless child.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Understanding she was a killer of children, Paramātmā closed his lotus eyes since he displayed all actions suitable to a small child. He did this in order to show fear of her by acting as a small child, in order to avoid seeing such an evil person, in order to avoid overpowering her with his glance, in order to hide his embarrassment at on killing a woman even though she would be greatly benefited--since he was an ocean of good qualities-- and in order to avoid seeing the ghastliness of her death. She placed him on her lap, though he was Ananta, who could kill persons like her, like a person who thinks something is a rope but which is the opposite (abuddhi).

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    She was a demon killer of children (bala-graham). The word bāla-grahaḥ is not in the feminine since it belongs to a class of words which retain their original gender when used as an adjective (even if the person is female.) He had his eyes closed because he was very small and afraid. Or she would not be able to see his eyes directly. Or since he was an ocean of good qualities he was ashamed of killing her, even though it was beneficial for her. Or he could bear to see her killed, since she was wearing clothing like Rohiṇī.

    Personal Guidance
    Like Pūtanā mistaking a sleeping snake for a rope, we keep clasping what will undo us — only Kṛṣṇa, the ananta, is safe to embrace.
    Practical Applications
    • Name one 'rope' you are presently nursing in your lap — a habit, ambition, or relationship — and ask honestly whether it is in fact antakam, a slow annihilation dressed as comfort.
    • Refuse the artificial affection that masks viṣaya: when someone or something offers sweetness with a hidden agenda, do not return the embrace, even politely.
    • Before sleep tonight, place on your 'lap' only Kṛṣṇa — chant His names with closed eyes, as the infant Kṛṣṇa Himself did, surrendering the day to the ananta who cannot be killed.
    Analogy

    Pūtanā lifting baby Kṛṣṇa onto her lap is the fool who picks up a coiled cobra in the dark and lets it lie in his cloth, certain it is the rope he dropped. Every soul that hugs a worldly pleasure to its chest does the same — mistaking antakam, that which will end us, for a comfort.

    Stories
    Aghāsura swallowing the cowherd boys

    Later in this same canto (SB 10.12), the python-demon Aghāsura opens his vast pink mouth and the cowherd boys, mistaking it for a beautiful mountain cave, walk in laughing. The 'cave' is in fact death.

    Pūtanā in 10.6.8 makes the same kind of error in reverse: she thinks she has placed a target on her lap; she has actually placed her own antaka. Whoever does not see Kṛṣṇa for who He is — whether by demon-eyes or by attached eyes — will end up handling Him as though He were something else.

    Hiraṇyakaśipu and the pillar

    The asura-king, sure that no being could touch him, mocked Prahlāda's claim that Hari was everywhere — even in this pillar? (SB 7.8). He struck the pillar; out came Nṛsiṁha. He had been standing all along beside his antaka, calling it furniture.

    Kṛṣṇa on Pūtanā's lap is the same secret in miniature: the demon clasps the destroyer and calls Him 'baby.'

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.9

    तां तीक्ष्णचित्तामतिवामचेष्टितां वीक्ष्यान्तरा कोषपरिच्छदासिवत् । वरस्त्रियं तत्प्रभया च धर्षिते निरीक्ष्यमाणे जननी ह्यतिष्ठताम् ॥ ९ ॥

    Important Words
    • koṣa - paricchada - asi - vat — like a sharp sword (asi) in a soft sheath (koṣa-paricchada)
      Compound — koṣa (scabbard) + paricchada (covering) + asi (sword) + vat (like). The Bhāgavata's signature image: outer softness, inner edge. Used by Gauḍīya ācāryas as a master diagnostic for sat-saṅga vs. asat-saṅga — judge by the steel, not the cloth.
    • tīkṣṇa - cittām — having a very fierce (lit. sharp) heart
      Compound — tīkṣṇa (sharp) + citta (mind, heart). The inner reality the sheath conceals. The verse pointedly places tīkṣṇa-citta and ati-vāma-ceṣṭitā (very pleasing behavior) side by side — the same person is both, and only Kṛṣṇa-tattva can keep the two clear.
    • ati - vāma - ceṣṭitām — (although she was) performing very pleasing actions
      Compound — ati (very) + vāma (pleasant, lovely) + ceṣṭita (behavior). The cosmetic surface. The gopīs' restraint is not naïveté in itself — they are reading the ceṣṭita honestly; the lesson is that ceṣṭita alone is not a reliable index of citta.
    • vara - striyam — the very beautiful woman
      Compound — vara (excellent) + strī (woman). The form, again, is impeccable. Bhāgavata refuses to deny the beauty; it simply refuses to let beauty close the case.
    • tat - prabhayā — by her influence / brilliance
      Compound — tat (her) + prabhā (radiance). A semi-magical influence accompanies the disguise — what the purport will call dharṣita, an overwhelming. Mahā-māyā can briefly hold sober devotees still by a mood-influence; only direct Kṛṣṇa-smaraṇa breaks the spell.
    Pūtanā Rākṣasī’s heart was fierce and cruel, but she looked like a very affectionate mother. Thus she resembled a sharp sword in a soft sheath. Although seeing her within the room, Yaśodā and Rohiṇī, overwhelmed by her beauty, did not stop her, but remained silent because she treated the child like a mother.
    Purport

    Although Pūtanā was an outsider and although she personified fierce death because the determination within her heart was to kill the child, when she directly came and placed the child on her lap to offer the child her breast to suck, the mothers were so captivated by her beauty that they did not prohibit her. Sometimes a beautiful woman is dangerous because everyone, being captivated by external beauty ( māyā-mohita ), is unable to understand what is in her mind. Those who are captivated by the beauty of the external energy are called māyā-mohita. Mohitaṁ nābhijānāti mām ebhyaḥ param avyayam ( Bg. 7.13 ). Na te viduḥ svārtha-gatiṁ hi viṣṇuṁ durāśayā ye bahir-artha-māninaḥ ( Bhāg. 7.5.31 ). Here, of course, the two mothers Rohiṇī and Yaśodā were not māyā-mohita, deluded by the external energy, but to develop the pastimes of the Lord, they were captivated by Yoga-māyā. Such māyā-moha is the action of Yoga-māyā.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Pūtanā Rākṣasī's heart was fierce and cruel, but she looked like a very affectionate mother. Thus she resembled a sharp sword in a soft sheath. Although seeing her within the room, Yaśodā and Rohiṇī, overwhelmed by her beauty, did not stop her, but remained silent because she treated the child like a mother.
    Commentary

    Why did Yasoda and Rohini not stop Putana? This verse answers the question. She acted like a very attentive mother. Though inside inwardly she was cruel, externally she was gentle. She was like a sharp knife in a soft leather sheath. Yasoda and Rohini were overwhelmed by her influence (tat prabhaya), thinking that Ambika, Indrani or Laksmi of the three worlds had come to feed Krsna out of motherly love. Thus they stood there watching her, not preventing her from holding the child.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Instead of ati sometimes atha (meaning “very much” in this case) is seen. It modifies both phrases. Pūtanā had a very cruel heart and very gentle conduct. Because of her gentle exterior her real motive could not be detected. Because she came within the house, the two mothers did not detect her motive, since they could not conceive that an evil person would suddenly enter a stranger’s house. They had no doubts because she appeared to be the best of women. They were overpowered by the power of her show of affection (tat-prabhayā). The word jananī (singular) is used for the dual number by poetic license, following rules like Pāṇini 7.1.39.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    They saw her in the room, near the child (antarā), as the best of women, though she had a cruel heart and had crooked activities (vāma-ceṣṭitām). Though they saw her, they just stood there. They did not stop her because they were bewildered (dharṣite) by her beauty, since she was affectionate like a mother (prabhayā), or they were overcome by the Lord’s power, since he wanted to suck Pūtanā’s life air. Otherwise they would have prevented her and she would not be able to put Kṛṣṇa on her lap. Or though cruel hearted, she had charming actions like a mother (vāma-ceṣṭitām). She did not seem wicked. Evil persons cannot suddenly enter others’ houses. They had faith that she was a good woman. Thus they did not do anything, overpowered by her beauty and charm. She was capable of doing her actions, like a knife in a sheath.

    Maya

      His Holiness Sridhar Swami· 2001-04-25· English
    Personal Guidance
    Outer beauty can hide inner poison; without Kṛṣṇa-conscious discrimination, even mothers stay silent before a sword in a soft sheath.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice today where you are being swayed by surface charm — a person, a feed, a pitch — and pause to ask what intention sits beneath the sheath.
    • Refuse to mistake māyā-mohita politeness for protection; speak up when something feels off around a child, a junior, or yourself, even if the form looks pleasing.
    • Each evening, take shelter of Kṛṣṇa through japa or Gītā 7.13, asking Him to be the Yoga-māyā that guards your home and heart from cruelty dressed as care.
    Analogy

    Pūtanā among the gopīs is a sharp sword sleeping in a velvet sheath — the velvet is so soft that two loving mothers pick it up and lay it on the cradle. Without Kṛṣṇa-conscious discrimination, even the sober gopī cannot tell that the affection on the surface is only the cloth wrapped around the blade.

    Stories
    Manthara whispering to Kaikeyī

    Old, hunch-backed, and posing as Kaikeyī's most loving servant, Manthara entered the queen's apartments in Ayodhyā (Rāmāyaṇa, Ayodhyā-kāṇḍa) and dripped a slow poison of suspicion against Rāma. She wore the velvet of long service; her words were the asi inside.

    Kaikeyī, like Yaśodā and Rohiṇī in 10.6.9, did not stop her — partly because the form was familiar, partly because the smile was tender. The verse warns: when the sheath is soft enough, even sincere love can fail to draw the sword in time.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.10

    ↗
    तस्मिन् स्तनं दुर्जरवीर्यमुल्बणं घोराङ्कमादाय शिशोर्ददावथ । गाढं कराभ्यां भगवान् प्रपीड्य तत्- प्राणै: समं रोषसमन्वितोऽपिबत् ॥ १० ॥
    tasmin stanaṁ durjara-vīryam ulbaṇaṁ
    [tasmin stanam durjara-veeryam ulbanam]
    ghorāṅkam ādāya śiśor dadāv atha
    [ghoraankam aadaaya shishor dadaav atha]
    gāḍhaṁ karābhyāṁ bhagavān prapīḍya tat-
    [gaadham karaabhyaam bhagavaan prapeedya tat-]
    prāṇaiḥ samaṁ roṣa-samanvito ’pibat
    [praanaih samam rosha-samanvito ’pibat]

    Important Words
    • bhagavān — the Supreme Personality of Godhead
      Nominative — the technical title (one in possession of the six aiśvaryas in full). The verse uses it precisely at the moment of action: the infant who has been allowing Himself to be held is acting now as Bhagavān, the full Person, not as a 'mystic baby.' Gauḍīya doctrine: bhagavān is the highest realization of the Absolute, above Brahman and Paramātmā.
    • roṣa - samanvitaḥ — filled with anger
      Compound — roṣa (wrath) + samanvita (endowed with). Krodha is one of Bhagavān's emotional kāyas — His anger at demons is itself His mercy to devotees. Gauḍīya rasa-śāstra distinguishes raudra-rasa toward asuras from vātsalya-rasa toward Vraja; both flow from the same Person.
    • prapīḍya — pressing hard
      Gerund — having squeezed. The verb is intentional and total; the Lord finishes what He starts. In Pūtanā's case the squeeze removes both viṣa and life — viṣaya and ahaṅkāra in one motion, as the ācāryas read it.
    • durjara - vīryam — a very powerful (poison-)weapon
      Compound — durjara (hard to digest, hard to overcome) + vīrya (potency). The poison was designed to kill instantly; Kṛṣṇa drinks it as if it were milk. He alone can digest what is durjara because He is the original durjara-jit.
    • apibat — sucked (the breast)
      Aorist of pā (to drink). The same verb that will later describe His drinking the gopīs' affectionate milk in 10.6.39. The verse holds the two side by side: He drinks the demon's poison and the mother's love with the same lips, and only the bhāva of the offering decides which side it falls on.
    On that very spot, the fiercely dangerous Rākṣasī took Kṛṣṇa on her lap and pushed her breast into His mouth. The nipple of her breast was smeared with a dangerous, immediately effective poison, but the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Kṛṣṇa, becoming very angry at her, took hold of her breast, squeezed it very hard with both hands, and sucked out both the poison and her life.
    Purport

    Lord Kṛṣṇa was not angry at Pūtanā for His own sake. Rather, He was angry because the Rākṣasī had killed so many small children in Vrajabhūmi. Therefore He decided that she should be punished by having to forfeit her life.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    On that very spot, the fiercely dangerous Rākṣasī took Kṛṣṇa on her lap and pushed her breast into His mouth. The nipple of her breast was smeared with a dangerous, immediately effective poison, but the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Kṛṣṇa, becoming very angry at her, took hold of her breast, squeezed it very hard with both hands, and sucked out both the poison and her life.
    Commentary

    In that place (tasmin) Putana (ghora—fierce woman) offered to the baby (sisoh) her breast covered with poison (durjaram viryam). He held her breast so tightly with his two hands that she could not pull him away (gadham prapidya). Filled with anger that she was going to kill all the children of Vraja by offering her breast to them, his sakti of destruction (samhara sakti) sucked out her impure life. He did not personally do this. Though we say a man cuts down the tree, actually it is the axe held by the man that cuts down the tree. Similarly it is stated that Krsna sucked out her life airs, though actually it was his samhara sakti that performed the work.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    She performed her action at that place. This shows her boldness. The poison (vīryam) was so intense (ulbaṇam) that just touching it would cause death. Śrīdhara Svāmī explains this verse. She pulled him to her lap. The verb has two objects (him and the lap). Angry at that breast since it had arrived in Vraja to kill all the babies, he drank the milk along with her life airs, since it was suitable to kill by deception since she was also deceptive. He became angry in order to drink her life airs from her breast. The power of his anger (not Kṛṣṇa personally) sucked out her impure, evil propensities, just as a man with an axe cuts down a tree. He only pretended to suck her breast. The results she would attain were because of this imitation of sucking of her breast. This anger was also temporary, and then transformed into the greatest mercy. To indicate this, Kṛṣṇa is called Bhagavān, filled with qualities like compassion which never leave him. This will be revealed later. Since he was absorbed in his infant pastimes, his śakti acted on his behalf. All his śaktis performed appropriately since they were waiting for opportunities to serve while he was absorbed in his infant mood. Śrīdhara Svāmī says that Kṛṣṇa understood that this poison was not suitable for food. Poison cannot kill a person filled already filled with poison (Pūtanā). Therefore he thought “I will mix her life airs with mine in order to that they become purified by association.”

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    At that time or in that place (tasmin), she offered her breast, which had unrelenting (durjara), powerful, sharp (ulbanam) poison. Just by touching it one would die. The Lord, full of qualities like mercy, but also greatly angry at the number of infants she had previously killed, pressed her forcibly against his chest. Or he drank as if angry, since in drinking her life air he was most merciful to her. This becomes clear later.

      His Holiness Sridhar Swami· 2001-04-26· English
    Personal Guidance
    Kṛṣṇa's wrath at Pūtanā is real compassion — He punishes the destroyer of children, teaching us that true mercy protects souls, not appearances.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice today where you confuse niceness with compassion, and refuse to stay silent when a real harm is being done to someone's spiritual welfare.
    • Take one hidden 'poisoned offering' in your life — flattery, indulgence, distraction dressed as care — and squeeze it dry by naming it honestly to yourself before the Lord.
    • Speak to or pray for a child today with the awareness that Kṛṣṇa Himself defends the innocent; commit one concrete act to protect the young in your circle.
    Analogy

    Kṛṣṇa angrily seizing Pūtanā's poisoned breast is the surgeon who finds the abscess and lances it on the spot — not gently, because gentleness here would be cruelty to the child still in the room. His krodha is not the temper of a slighted infant; it is the same krodha that drew Sudarśana on Ambarīṣa's behalf.

    Stories
    Nṛsiṁhadeva on the threshold

    When Prahlāda's torments reached their limit, the Lord burst out of the pillar as Nṛsiṁha (SB 7.8), placed Hiraṇyakaśipu on His own lap, and tore him open at the precise twilight on the precise threshold — a krodha so exact that the universe trembled, yet Prahlāda alone could approach Him and calm Him.

    The baby on Pūtanā's lap and Nṛsiṁha on His own lap are the same Lord in two postures: His wrath at child-killers is never anger in the human sense — it is the protective fire of a Bhagavān who refuses to let the innocent be ground under.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.11

    ↗
    सा मुञ्च मुञ्चालमिति प्रभाषिणी निष्पीड्यमानाखिलजीवमर्मणि । विवृत्य नेत्रे चरणौ भुजौ मुहु: प्रस्विन्नगात्रा क्षिपती रुरोद ह ॥ ११ ॥
    sā muñca muñcālam iti prabhāṣiṇī
    [saa muncha munchaalam iti prabhaashinee]
    niṣpīḍyamānākhila-jīva-marmaṇi
    [nishpeedyamaanaakhila-jeeva-marmani]
    vivṛtya netre caraṇau bhujau muhuḥ
    [vivritya netre charanau bhujau muhuh]

    Important Words
    • akhila - jīva - marmaṇi — in every center of her vitality
      Compound — akhila (all) + jīva (living force) + marman (vital point, locative plural). Vedic anatomy names 107 marmas; Kṛṣṇa presses all of them simultaneously. The verse insists that Pūtanā's annihilation is total, not symbolic — there is no part of her false-mother form left to regroup.
    • muñca — give up / leave (me)
      Imperative of muc (to release). Repeated for force. The same root underlies mokṣa — and indeed by being unable to make Kṛṣṇa muñca her, she ends up receiving mokṣa from Him in verse 35. The verb she shouts at Him becomes the gift He gives her.
    • niṣpīḍyamānā — being severely pressed
      Passive present participle of niṣpīḍ (to press out, squeeze). The grammar is passive — she is not acting, she is being acted upon. Māyā's whole stance reverses: from agent to patient, from holder to held.
    • prasvinna - gātrā — with her body perspiring
      Compound — prasvinna (perspiring) + gātra (body, feminine). Bhāgavata catalogs the involuntary signs of breakdown — sweat, wide eyes, flailing limbs — to make the unmasking complete and undeniable. The mother-disguise drips off her in sweat.
    • ruroda — cried severely
      Perfect of rud (to weep, wail). The Bhāgavata gives the demon her actual voice for the first time — and it is a wail, not the seductive lullaby of verses 5–7. Speech tells the truth where appearance lied.
    Unbearably pressed in every vital point, the demon Pūtanā began to cry, “Please leave me, leave me! Suck my breast no longer!” Perspiring, her eyes wide open and her arms and legs flailing, she cried very loudly again and again.
    Purport

    The Rākṣasī was severely punished by Kṛṣṇa. She threw her arms and legs about, and Kṛṣṇa also began to kick her with His legs to punish her properly for her mischievous activities.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Unbearably pressed in every vital point, the demon Pūtanā began to cry, "Please leave me, leave me! Suck my breast no longer!" Perspiring, her eyes wide open and her arms and legs flailing, she cried very loudly again and again.
    Commentary

    Being squeezed by the child in all her sensitive points, she began flailing her hands and legs.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    After saying “get away from me” she could not repeat it since she had no strength to utter any more. He pressed all the points (marma) containing life in her body. She cried out in pain, while crying. The word ha expresses joy.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    She shouted loudly, but uselessly (alam). He pressed all the points where life resided in her. She cried loudly. Ha expresses bliss.

    • Sridhar Sw SB 10-06-11-12 - Vraja bhakti - Ljubljana 2001-04-27
      His Holiness Sridhar Swami· 2001-04-27· English
    Personal Guidance
    Even the demoness Pūtanā, struck at every vital point by Kṛṣṇa, must cry out and release her grip — what we cling to in māyā cannot withstand His touch.
    Practical Applications
    • Name one viṣaya you have been 'feeding' as Pūtanā fed Kṛṣṇa, and today refuse it one concrete time — skip the scroll, the snack, the second look.
    • When an attachment screams 'leave me, leave me!' as it loosens, do not rescue it; let Kṛṣṇa finish the work and chant His names through the discomfort.
    • Before sleep, offer your day's vital points — ambitions, fears, resentments — to Kṛṣṇa's feet, asking Him to press out whatever is not bhakti.
    Analogy

    Pūtanā shrieking 'muñca, muñca' is the householder addicted to a viṣaya finally crying for it to release him — not the other way around. By the time Kṛṣṇa squeezes the vital points, the chain that looked like the demon's grip on Him is revealed to be Kṛṣṇa's grip on her, and she begs for the very freedom she was sure she had taken.

    Stories
    Ajāmila at the moment of death

    The fallen brāhmaṇa Ajāmila (SB 6.1–3), in his last agony, called his son Nārāyaṇa's name — and the Viṣṇudūtas appeared. He had not meant to chant; the Yamadūtas were already pressing on his vital points (marma). The cry came out anyway, and Hari was bound by His own name.

    Pūtanā's 'muñca, muñca' is the bookend: she too is in marmaṇi pressure, she too cries out at the very person whose name is destroying and liberating her in one motion. Even the unwanted scream toward Kṛṣṇa is not wasted.

    📝 My note

    petuḥ
    fell down
    kṣitau
    on the surface of the world
    vajra - nipāta - śaṅkayā
    by suspecting that thunderbolts were falling .
    Important Words
    • svanena — by the vibration of the sound
      Instrumental of svana (sound). Sound is the primary disturber and the primary protector in Bhāgavata — the demon's svana shakes the worlds; the Lord's nāma-svana (verse 28) calms them. Two sounds, opposite effects.
    • vajra - nipāta - śaṅkayā — by the fear that thunderbolts were falling
      Compound — vajra (thunderbolt) + nipāta (falling) + śaṅkā (suspicion). The cosmic register: even Indra's weapon-sound is the closest analogue people can find for a Rākṣasī's death-howl. The verse silently signals what is coming — by 10.6.13, she will fall 'like Vṛtrāsura struck by vajra.'
    • ati gabhīra raṁhasā — very deep and forceful
      These adjacent modifiers — ati (very) + gabhīra (deep) + raṁhasā (with force) — stack to convey scale. Bhāgavata likes to set up cosmic disturbance in one verse only to set Kṛṣṇa's quiet unconcern against it in the next.
    • cacāla — trembled
      Perfect of cal (to move, shake). The earth's trembling is the standard Purāṇic signature of a demon's death — used for Hiraṇyakaśipu, Vṛtra, Kaṁsa. The verse situates Pūtanā in that line.
    • pratinedire — (the directions) vibrated
      Perfect middle plural of prati-nad (to resound). Directions themselves echo back the death-cry. In Gauḍīya cosmography, dik (direction) and grahas (planets) are personalities; they too are 'witnessing' the Lord's first public act in Vraja.
    As Pūtanā screamed loudly and forcefully, the earth with its mountains, and outer space with its planets, trembled. The lower planets and all directions vibrated, and people fell down, fearing that thunderbolts were falling upon them.
    Purport

    Śrīla Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura remarks that in this verse the word rasā refers to the planetary systems below the earth, such as Rasātala, Atala, Vitala, Sutala and Talātala.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    As Pūtanā screamed loudly and forcefully, the earth with its mountains, and outer space with its planets, trembled. The lower planets and all directions vibrated, and people fell down, fearing that thunderbolts were falling upon them.
    Commentary

    Rasa means rasatala and other lower planets.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The earth and lower planets trembled and echoed the sound, and the directions echoed the sound. The people had never heard such a loud sound and thus fell to the ground.

    • PSarasvati Sw SB 10-06-12 Hindi - 2026-03-10 ISKCON Vrindavan
      His Holiness Prabodhananda Sarasvati Swami· 2026-03-10· Hindi
    Personal Guidance
    Pūtanā's scream shakes the three worlds, yet baby Kṛṣṇa is unmoved — surrender to Him is the one shelter when reality itself seems to tremble.
    Practical Applications
    • When today's news or a personal crisis makes the ground feel like it's shaking, pause and chant Kṛṣṇa's names before reacting.
    • Notice where you give cosmic weight to a 'scream' — a boss's anger, a market drop, a diagnosis — and consciously place that fear at Kṛṣṇa's feet instead.
    • Refuse to mistake loud disturbances for ultimate reality; return to one fixed sādhana (japa, reading, offering) that does not bend to the noise.
    Analogy

    Pūtanā's death-cry shaking the three worlds while baby Kṛṣṇa rests on her chest is like a thunderclap rolling across a kitchen courtyard where a lamp is still burning steadily on the windowsill — the room is full of noise, but the flame does not even bend. Saraṇāgati is that flame.

    Stories
    Hanumān at the burning of Laṅkā

    When Hanumān torched Rāvaṇa's city (Rāmāyaṇa, Sundara-kāṇḍa), the heavens roared, citizens screamed, monsters ran. Sītā, in the Aśoka-vana, kept Her mind on Rāma alone. The thunder of the worlds did not move Her one breath because Her shelter was elsewhere.

    The baby Kṛṣṇa unmoved on Pūtanā's chest in 10.6.12 is the cosmic-scale form of the same shelter: when one is fixed in Bhagavān, the screams of māyā remain mere weather.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.13

    ↗
    निशाचरीत्थं व्यथितस्तना व्यसु- र्व्यादाय केशांश्चरणौ भुजावपि । प्रसार्य गोष्ठे निजरूपमास्थिता वज्राहतो वृत्र इवापतन्नृप ॥ १३ ॥

    Important Words
    • vyasuḥ — lost her life
      Compound — vi (away) + asu (vital breath). The breath has been driven out at the marmas. In the Bhāgavata's economy, even this losing of breath is, paradoxically, a winning — verses 35–36 reveal that her contact with Kṛṣṇa's mouth has already done the deeper work.
    • nija - rūpam āsthitā — (she) assumed her original form
      Compound — nija (own) + rūpa (form) + āsthitā (situated). The māyā-yoṣit collapses; the actual rūpa appears. Acintya-bhedābheda corollary applied to māyā: the false form had real consequences, but the moment of grace forces the genuine rūpa back into view.
    • vajra - āhataḥ vṛtraḥ iva — as if (she fell) like Vṛtrāsura struck by the thunderbolt
      Bhāgavata frequently locates a new episode by simile to a famous earlier one. Vṛtra-vadha is the paradigm of asura-collapse; Pūtanā's death is being entered into that lineage. Adjacent tokens vajra-āhataḥ + vṛtraḥ + iva can be cited together as the simile-unit.
    • goṣṭhe — in the pasturing ground
      Locative of goṣṭha (cow-enclosure, pasture). She falls in Vraja's own pastureland — the most intimate Kṛṣṇa-soil. Even her corpse, by location alone, is already in the field of His mercy.
    • niśā - carī — the Rākṣasī (lit. night-wanderer)
      Compound — niśā (night) + cara (mover). Standard Bhāgavata term for rākṣasīs, who do their work in darkness. Verses 5–7 dressed her as a daylight beauty; verse 13 returns her to her actual category. Māyā is always a night-mover even when it borrows the sun's costume.
    In this way the demon Pūtanā, very much aggrieved because her breast was being attacked by Kṛṣṇa, lost her life. O King Parīkṣit, opening her mouth wide and spreading her arms, legs and hair, she fell down in the pasturing ground in her original form as a Rākṣasī, as Vṛtrāsura had fallen when killed by the thunderbolt of Indra.
    Purport

    Pūtanā was a great Rākṣasī who knew the art of covering her original form by mystic power, but when she was killed her mystic power could not hide her, and she appeared in her original form.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    In this way the demon Pūtanā, very much aggrieved because her breast was being attacked by Kṛṣṇa, lost her life. O King Parīkṣit, opening her mouth wide and spreading her arms, legs and hair, she fell down in the pasturing ground in her original form as a Rākṣasī, as Vṛtrāsura had fallen when killed by the thunderbolt of Indra.
    Commentary

    Because of the pain of death, the raksasi could not maintain her disguise as a beautiful woman and reverted to her raksasi form.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Her original form was that of an owl. This is mentioned previously. Tokena jīva-haraṇaṁ yad ulūki-kāyāḥ: he killed the woman with the body of an owl when he was a child. (SB 1.2.27) The word goṣṭhe here means “near a place kept for milking the cows, not where people were living.” Moving her feet, she crushed trees. She flailed her arms as if trying to fly like a bird. It was previously mentioned that she flew to Vraja. O king! Understanding this story, you will protect the people (nṛpa).

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The pain in her breast was the main cause of her dying. She is compared to Vṛtra because she had a huge body and like him attained the spiritual world. Śukadeva calls to the king in joy. Or like you, a king, who protects people from the wicked, Kṛṣṇa protected Vraja from the body of Pūtanā.

    Her original form was that of an owl. This is mentioned previously. Tokena jīva-haraṇaṁ yad ulūki-kāyāḥ: he killed the woman with the body of an owl when he was a child. (SB 1.2.27) The word goṣṭhe here means “near a place kept for milking the cows, not where people were living.” Moving her feet, she crushed trees. She flailed her arms as if trying to fly like a bird. It was previously mentioned that she flew to Vraja. O king! Understanding this story, you will protect the people (nṛpa).

    Personal Guidance
    Māyā's disguises collapse at the touch of Kṛṣṇa — what looks nourishing but conceals death is exposed the moment He is allowed to act.
    Practical Applications
    • Name one 'Pūtanā' in your life today — a pleasure, feed, or relationship that wears a maternal face but quietly drains you — and cut contact with it before nightfall.
    • Before accepting any offering today (food, advice, attention), pause and ask whose interest it actually serves, then refuse what cannot be offered to Kṛṣṇa first.
    • When a glamour you have been chasing falls apart, refuse the urge to rebuild it; sit with the exposed Rākṣasī-form and thank Kṛṣṇa for the unmasking.
    Analogy

    Pūtanā's costume falls off as she dies, and what hits the ground in her true rākṣasī form is what we ourselves look like in māyā when grace finally rips the disguise. Like Vṛtra falling at the touch of Indra's vajra, every demoniac pretense in us falls at the touch of Kṛṣṇa — not gradually, but at one stroke once He chooses to act.

    Stories
    Vṛtrāsura's actual heart

    Bhāgavatam (SB 6.10–12) records that the very Vṛtra slain by Indra's thunderbolt was in his previous life Citraketu, an exalted devotee. Even his demoniac form, when at last cut down, revealed a soul who praised Hari with his dying breath.

    The verse compares Pūtanā's collapse to Vṛtra's, and the parallel is not only cosmic in scale — it also quietly foreshadows verses 35–36, where this Rākṣasī, killed by Kṛṣṇa, attains the position of a Vaikuṇṭha mother. The vajra of bhakti looks like destruction; it is in fact deliverance.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.14

    ↗
    पतमानोऽपि तद्देहस्त्रिगव्यूत्यन्तरद्रुमान् । चूर्णयामास राजेन्द्र महदासीत्तदद्भ‍ुतम् ॥ १४ ॥
    patamāno ’pi tad-dehas
    [patamaano ’pi tad-dehas]
    tri-gavyūty-antara-drumān
    [tri-gavyooty-antara-drumaan]
    cūrṇayām āsa rājendra
    [choornayaam aasa raajendra]
    mahad āsīt tad adbhutam
    [mahad aaseet tad adbhutam]
    Word-for-word
    patamānaḥ api — even while falling down tat - dehaḥ — her gigantic body tri - gavyūti - antara — within a limit of twelve miles drumān — all kinds of trees cūrṇayām āsa — smashed rājendra — O King Parīkṣit mahat āsīt — was quite gigantic tat — that body adbhutam — and very, very wonderful .
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    patamāno ’pi tad-dehas
    patamānaḥ api
    even while falling down
    tat - dehaḥ
    her gigantic body
    tri-gavyūty-antara-drumān

    within a limit of twelve miles
    drumān
