    was quite gigantic
    tat
    that body
    adbhutam
    and very, very wonderful .
    Important Words
    • tri - gavyūti - antara — within a limit of twelve miles
      Compound — tri (three) + gavyūti (a Vedic unit, roughly four miles, lit. the range of a cow's lowing) + antara (within). The pasture-unit measures the demon's scale: every Vrajavāsī's ear and ax-blow will be involved in the cleanup.
    • drumān — trees
      Accusative plural of druma. The trees of Vraja are devotees in tree-form per later cantos (SB 10.21, the gopīs' praise of Vṛndāvana). Their being crushed under Pūtanā's body is, on one level, the Vrajavāsīs themselves shaken; on another, it is the visible proof for them that an asura had walked among them.
    • cūrṇayām āsa — smashed (to powder)
      Periphrastic perfect of cūrṇaya (to pulverize). Even the falling of the demon-body is destructive — Bhāgavata refuses to romanticize her exit. And yet verse 41 will tell us the burning of that same body produced aguru fragrance: the Lord transmutes even her wreckage.
    • adbhutam — wonderful, extraordinary
      Neuter — modifies tat (that body). The same word the gopīs use for Kṛṣṇa's deeds (verse 18). Bhāgavata holds the two senses together: the demon was extraordinary, the Lord more so.
    • mahat āsīt — was quite gigantic
      Phrase — mahat (great) + āsīt (was). Adjacent tokens used to register pure scale, with a subtle note: even mahat in the demoniac sense is finite, and finite things fall when the Unlimited (anantam, 10.6.8) takes off His shoes and stands up.
    O King Parīkṣit, when the gigantic body of Pūtanā fell to the ground, it smashed all the trees within a limit of twelve miles. Appearing in a gigantic body, she was certainly extraordinary.
    Purport

    Because of the grievous hurt imposed upon her by Kṛṣṇa’s sucking her breast, Pūtanā, while dying, not only left the room but abandoned the village and fell down in the pasturing ground in her gigantic body.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    O King Parīkṣit, when the gigantic body of Pūtanā fell to the ground, it smashed all the trees within a limit of twelve miles. Appearing in a gigantic body, she was certainly extraordinary.
    Commentary

    Because of the pain, Putana ran out of the house, passed out of the village and fell on the outskirts. The word "api" is used here to signify that not only while living, but even in dying, she was killing other living entities. All the trees within an area of six krosas were crushed. It was remarkable that only the trees were crushed, her body having avoided crushing the houses of the village. These trees belonged to Kamsa’s garden, whose fruits were for his enjoyment, according to Vaisvana Tosini (Jiva’s commentary).

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Other astonishing facts are mentioned. Not only while living did she kill others, but in dying also she killed the trees all around for six krośas. This is signified by the word api. It was most astonishing that she crushed the trees for such a distance. Śukadeva addresses Parīkṣit as king of kings so that he can understand this and the glory of the Lord’s līlā-śakti.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Other astonishing facts are mentioned. Not only while living did she kill others, but in dying also while falling (patamāno ‘pi) she killed the trees all around for six krośas. Or even in falling she was in great pain. Or even in that body (tad-dehaḥ api), she was killing entities because the body was huge. She crushed the trees for six krośas, but not other living beings. That was most astonishing. O king! Because you are a great king you should believe this.

      His Holiness Prabodhananda Sarasvati Swami· 2013-10-12· Hindi
    Personal Guidance
    Pūtanā's gigantic, tree-smashing corpse shows what false motherhood truly is — yet even she fell within Kṛṣṇa's range; demoniac scale dies at His touch.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice today where you are impressed by sheer scale — wealth, follower counts, institutional size — and remember that Pūtanā too was 'extraordinary.'
    • Refuse to confuse outward magnitude with spiritual potency; measure the people and projects in your life by their relation to Kṛṣṇa, not their footprint.
    • When a 'gigantic' fear or obstacle looms in your day, recall that it falls within Kṛṣṇa's twelve-mile pasture — name it aloud and continue your sādhana.
    Analogy

    Pūtanā's twelve-mile corpse flattening the trees of Vraja is the demon-mountain that towered over your sādhana for years finally toppling into a single afternoon — every habit and association that grew around it splintering at once. What looked like a permanent feature of the landscape was always living on borrowed time within Kṛṣṇa's pasture.

    Stories
    Kumbhakarṇa's fall

    When Kumbhakarṇa, Rāvaṇa's gigantic brother, finally fell to Rāma's arrows on the battlefield of Laṅkā (Rāmāyaṇa, Yuddha-kāṇḍa), his body crashed across formations, crushing chariots and ranks. His scale had terrified the vānaras; his fall pressed the lesson home: no extraordinary magnitude survives the Lord.

    Pūtanā's tri-gavyūti corpse plays the same note in Vraja's softer register. The wonder is not that she was huge — it is that something that huge fell within twelve miles of where the infant Kṛṣṇa was lying.

    Important Words
    • īṣā - mātra — (teeth) like the front of a plow
      Compound — īṣā (plow-shaft) + mātra (measure). Vedic agricultural imagery. The very tools that till Vraja's fields are weaponized in this comparison — domestic life, when distorted by māyā, can grow teeth.
    • andha - kūpa — (eye-sockets) like blind wells
      Compound — andha (blind) + kūpa (well). Blind wells, in Vedic practice, are wells one no longer draws from, often covered. The image suggests the cessation of vision in the demoniac form — and stands quietly against the alert, all-seeing Hṛṣīkeśa of verse 24.
    • śūnya - toya - hrada - udaram — abdomen like a dried-up lake
      Compound — śūnya (empty) + toya (water) + hrada (pool) + udara (belly). The Bhāgavata's deliberate gloss on the asuric belly: an exhausted reservoir, drained of life. The contrast is the Vrajavāsīs' own jīvanam, kept full by Kṛṣṇa.
    • raudram — very fierce
      Adjective from rudra (terrifying). Names the rasa of the moment — raudra-rasa, terror at the asura unmasked. In Gauḍīya rasa-vicāra raudra is a vibhāva-class normally reserved for descriptions of demons or of Kṛṣṇa's krodha against them; both senses converge here.
    • santatrasuḥ sma — (the gopas and gopīs) became frightened
      Perfect plural of saṁ-tras (to be very afraid) + sma. The Vrajavāsīs' bhaya is not a defect — it sharpens their dependence on Kṛṣṇa and prepares the protective mantras of 22–29. Bhaya rightly placed becomes śaraṇāgati.
    The Rākṣasī’s mouth was full of teeth, each resembling the front of a plow, her nostrils were deep like mountain caves, and her breasts resembled big slabs of stone fallen from a hill. Her scattered hair was the color of copper. The sockets of her eyes appeared like deep blind wells, her fearful thighs resembled the banks of a river, her arms, legs and feet seemed like big bridges, and her abdomen appeared like a dried-up lake. The hearts, ears and heads of the cowherd men and women were already shocked by the Rākṣasī’s screaming, and when they saw the fierce wonder of her body, they were even more frightened.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The Rākṣasī's mouth was full of teeth, each resembling the front of a plow, her nostrils were deep like mountain caves, and her breasts resembled big slabs of stone fallen from a hill. Her scattered hair was the color of copper. The sockets of her eyes appeared like deep blind wells, her fearful thighs resembled the banks of a river, her arms, legs and feet seemed like big bridges, and her abdomen appeared like a dried-up lake. The hearts, ears and heads of the cowherd men and women were already shocked by the Rākṣasī's screaming, and when they saw the fierce wonder of her body, they were even more frightened.
    Commentary

    Seeing her body, the cowherd men and women became frightened. Her teeth were as large as ploughshares. Her nostrils were as deep as mountain caves. Her waist was like the bank of a river. All of this was terrible to see. Her hands, feet and thighs were like bridges and her belly was like a dried up lake. Already having their hearts, ears and heads shattered by her sound, on seeing her form they became more frightened.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Her ferocious nature is described with eight characteristics. Her thighs were frightening. Previously her loud wailing was frightening and now everyone understood that she was the killer of children. Seeing her, the cowherds could understand she was a demon. They were frightened out of affection for Kṛṣṇa. Sma indicates that they became completely frightened. This was directly visible.

    Related values & verses

    • SDAP SB 10-06-15-18 - 2009-05-25 Mauritius
      His Grace Sankarshan Prabhu· 2009-05-25· English
    • Sarvabhauma Pr Hindi SB 10-06-15-18 - 2021
      His Grace Sarvabhauma Prabhu· Hindi
    Personal Guidance
    Pūtanā's monstrous form unmasks māyā: what dazzles or terrifies the senses is hollow — only Kṛṣṇa's shelter steadies the heart.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice one image today (news, scroll, gossip) that hijacks your heart through fear or fascination, and consciously withdraw the senses (vairāgya) back to Kṛṣṇa's name.
    • When dread rises in the body, refuse to let it dictate action — chant the holy name aloud and let abhaya, not adrenaline, decide your next step.
    • Speak to someone who is shaken by a frightening appearance in their life and gently point past the form to the shelter of Kṛṣṇa, not to mere reassurance.
    Analogy

    Pūtanā's body in its original form — teeth like plowshares, breasts like fallen boulders, an abdomen like a dried lake — is the inventory of māyā after Kṛṣṇa lifts the veil. The same components that, dressed and perfumed, drew the men of Vraja, now drive them to terror. Without Kṛṣṇa-conscious sight, we have been admiring the same boulders all along.

    Stories
    Bhīṣma's vision on the bed of arrows

    Lying pierced on śara-talpa during the last days of the Mahābhārata, Bhīṣma instructed Yudhiṣṭhira in the Śānti-parva and Anuśāsana-parva on what the body, the kingdom, and the world really are — temporary, decaying, and worthy only insofar as they are offered to Hari.

    Verses 15–17 of SB 10.6 give Vraja its own śara-talpa moment in compressed form: the Vrajavāsīs see, in one corpse, the truth about all bodies. After such a vision, only Kṛṣṇa is shelter, not form, not strength, not magnitude.

    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.18

    ↗
    बालं च तस्या उरसि क्रीडन्तमकुतोभयम् । गोप्यस्तूर्णं समभ्येत्य जगृहुर्जातसम्भ्रमा: ॥ १८ ॥
    bālaṁ ca tasyā urasi
    [baalam cha tasyaa urasi]

    Important Words
    • akutobhayam — without fear (from any quarter)
      Compound — a (not) + kutas (from where) + bhaya (fear). 'Fearless from any direction.' Used for the liberated soul in śruti and for Kṛṣṇa Himself here. Gauḍīya reading: jīva attains akutobhaya only by taking shelter of Bhagavān; Kṛṣṇa is akutobhaya by His own svarūpa.
    • krīḍantam — engaged in playing
      Present participle of krīḍ (to sport). The Bhāgavatam's signature word for divine līlā. Even on a corpse, He plays — līlā is His mode, not His mood. Sets up Govinda's protection of 'while playing' in verse 25.
    • gopyaḥ — the cowherd women
      Nominative plural. The Vraja gopīs as cradle-mothers — in Gauḍīya rasa-vicāra these are the proto-mātṛkās of vātsalya-rasa, distinct from (and in a sense the maternal background to) the future mādhurya-rasa of the same gopīs as Kṛṣṇa's parā-prakāśa lovers.
    • jāta - sambhramāḥ — (picked Him up) with great care and reverence
      Compound — jāta (arisen) + sambhrama (loving reverential agitation). Bhāgavata catches their bhāva exactly: not panic, not casual delight, but the trembling tenderness that arises when the unbearably precious has just been seen to be in danger. Pure vātsalya-rasa.
    • tasyāḥ urasi — on her (Pūtanā's) chest
      Adjacent locative — tasyāḥ (her, genitive) + urasi (on the chest, locative). The verse foregrounds the location: He plays on what was meant to kill Him. Gauḍīya commentators read this as His personal seal on what verses 35–36 will declare — that her chest, by His having lain on it, has become a Vaikuṇṭha-aṅga.
    Without fear, the child Kṛṣṇa was playing on the upper portion of Pūtanā Rākṣasī’s breast, and when the gopīs saw the child’s wonderful activities, they immediately came forward with great jubilation and picked Him up.
    Purport

    Here is the Supreme Personality of Godhead — Kṛṣṇa. Although the Rākṣasī Pūtanā could increase or decrease her bodily size by her mystic abilities and thus gain proportionate power, the Supreme Personality of Godhead is equally powerful in any transcendental form. Kṛṣṇa is the real Personality of Godhead because whether as a child or as a grown-up young man, He is the same person. He does not need to become powerful by meditation or any other external endeavor. Therefore when the greatly powerful Pūtanā expanded her body, Kṛṣṇa remained the same small child and fearlessly played on the upper portion of her breast. Ṣaḍaiśvarya-pūrṇa. Bhagavān, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, is always full in all potencies, regardless of whether He is present in this form or that. His potencies are always full. Parāsya śaktir vividhaiva śrūyate. He can display all potencies under any circumstances.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Without fear, the child Kṛṣṇa was playing on the upper portion of Pūtanā Rākṣasī's breast, and when the gopīs saw the child's wonderful activities, they immediately came forward with great jubilation and picked Him up.
    Commentary

    Putana’s chest was raised like a hill, suitable for playing on. Entering the maternity ward, the gopis could not see Krsna there (Yasoda and Rohini had fainted). Desiring to see him they went outside the house, and saw him playing on Putana.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The gopīs around Yaśodā, understanding that the demon killed children, left Yaśodā and Rohiṇī who had fainted and ran, took the child from the dead body, and returned to the house. Their affection for him increased on seeing that he was playing happily on her breast. Because he was child he had no fear. But actually he made others have no fear as well. Seeing him moving his hands and legs as if dancing while situated on her breast, they became astonished or overjoyed (jāta-sambhramāḥ). They then went directly (samabhyetya) and climbed up on the huge corpse lying on the ground and took the child away. Since they all had natural affection for Kṛṣṇa, they developed great power to do this. It will be described that they knew how to perform protective rituals with nyāsa. It should be understood that these gopīs were the same age as Yaśodā since they protected Kṛṣṇa with the affection of a mother.

      His Holiness Romapada Swami· 2018-09-03· English
    Personal Guidance
    Kṛṣṇa is ṣaḍaiśvarya-pūrṇa — fearless on Pūtanā's breast as on Vaikuṇṭha's throne; take shelter of His unchanging fullness, not your shifting strength.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice today where you try to manufacture abhaya through preparation or control, and instead take one small action grounded in dependence on Kṛṣṇa's protection.
    • When a 'Pūtanā' arrives in disguise — a sweet-seeming offer that would poison your sādhana — refuse it on the spot, trusting that Kṛṣṇa's presence inside you sees through it.
    • Before sleep, recall one moment from your day when you were 'small' and the situation was 'large,' and offer that moment to Kṛṣṇa as proof that His potency, not yours, carried you.
    Analogy

    Baby Kṛṣṇa playing fearlessly on the corpse-mountain that terrifies all of Vraja is the small, royal lamp left burning on the cremation ground at midnight — a single point of cheer that the cremation cannot put out. He is abhaya by His own constitution; ours is borrowed and only as bright as the wick of remembrance.

    Stories
    Prahlāda on Hiraṇyakaśipu's lap

    When the asura-emperor placed Prahlāda on his own lap with murder in his eyes (SB 7.5), the boy showed not a trace of fear. He even smiled. His abhaya did not come from confidence in his strength; it came from the Lord he had already taken shelter of.

    Kṛṣṇa on Pūtanā's chest in 10.6.18 is the source of Prahlāda's abhaya appearing in His own Person. The boy mirrored what the Lord shows here: fearlessness rooted in the constitutional fact that Bhagavān cannot be touched.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.19

    ↗
    यशोदारोहिणीभ्यां ता: समं बालस्य सर्वत: । रक्षां विदधिरे सम्यग्गोपुच्छभ्रमणादिभि: ॥ १९ ॥
    yaśodā-rohiṇībhyāṁ tāḥ
    [yashodaa-rohineebhyaam taah]
    samaṁ bālasya sarvataḥ
    [samam baalasya sarvatah]
    rakṣāṁ vidadhire samyag
    [rakshaam vidadhire samyag]
    go-puccha-bhramaṇādibhiḥ
    [go-puchcha-bhramanaadibhih]
    Word-for-word
    yaśodā - rohiṇībhyām — with mother Yaśodā and mother Rohiṇī, who principally took charge of the child tāḥ — the other gopīs samam — equally as important as Yaśodā and Rohiṇī bālasya — of the child sarvataḥ — from all dangers rakṣām — protection vidadhire — executed samyak — completely go - puccha - bhramaṇa - ādibhiḥ — by waving around the switch of a cow .
    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)
    yaśodā-rohiṇībhyāṁ tāḥ
    yaśodā - rohiṇībhyām
    with mother Yaśodā and mother Rohiṇī, who principally took charge of the child

    the other gopīs

    go-puccha-bhramaṇādibhiḥ

    by waving around the switch of a cow .
    Important Words
    • yaśodā - rohiṇībhyām — with mother Yaśodā and mother Rohiṇī
      Dual instrumental compound. The two principal mothers acting together — Vraja parenting is shared, not nuclear. In Gauḍīya bhakti-tattva, vātsalya-rasa often distributes itself across multiple mothers (Yaśodā, Rohiṇī, the gopīs collectively); the verse models this co-motherhood as proper bhakti-saṁskṛti.
    • go - puccha - bhramaṇa - ādibhiḥ — by waving around the switch of a cow, etc.
      Compound — go (cow) + puccha (tail) + bhramaṇa (rotation) + ādi (etc.). A traditional Vedic protective rite. Gauḍīya tradition treats go-rakṣya and dependence on cow-substances as devotional, not folkloric — the cow herself is dear to Kṛṣṇa (Govinda).
    • rakṣām — protection
      Accusative of rakṣā — the standard word for ritual protection (kavaca). Sets the keyword for verses 20–29: the entire pericope is one extended rakṣā-mantra over the child.
    • sarvataḥ — from all (sides)
      Indeclinable — 'in every direction.' The rakṣā is not point-specific but ambient, foreshadowing the 360-degree directional cover in verses 22–23.
    • samyak — completely
      Adverb. Vedic ritual is total or it is not yet ritual — partial protection is not protection. Same standard the verse will apply to mantra-nyāsa in 21–24.
    Thereafter, mother Yaśodā and Rohiṇī, along with the other elderly gopīs, waved about the switch of a cow to give full protection to the child Śrī Kṛṣṇa.
    Purport

    When Kṛṣṇa was saved from such a great danger, mother Yaśodā and Rohiṇī were principally concerned, and the other elderly gopīs, who were almost equally concerned, followed the activities of mother Yaśodā and Rohiṇī. Here we find that in household affairs, ladies could take charge of protecting a child simply by taking help from the cow. As described here, they knew how to wave about the switch of a cow so as to protect the child from all types of danger. There are so many facilities afforded by cow protection, but people have forgotten these arts. The importance of protecting cows is therefore stressed by Kṛṣṇa in Bhagavad-gītā ( kṛṣi-go-rakṣya-vāṇijyaṁ vaiśya-karma svabhāvajam ). Even now in the Indian villages surrounding Vṛndāvana, the villagers live happily simply by giving protection to the cow. They keep cow dung very carefully and dry it to use as fuel. They keep a sufficient stock of grains, and because of giving protection to the cows, they have sufficient milk and milk products to solve all economic problems. Simply by giving protection to the cow, the villagers live so peacefully. Even the urine and stool of cows have medicinal value.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Thereafter, mother Yaśodā and Rohiṇī, along with the other elderly gopīs, waved about the switch of a cow to give full protection to the child Śrī Kṛṣṇa.
    Commentary

    This verse mentions that the gopis, along with Yasoda and Rohini performed rituals of protection. Yasoda and Rohin did not take a leading role because they were extremely affected by anxiety for Krsna’s safety. They protected him in all limbs (sarvatah), by waving a cow tail and other items such as mustard seeds and touching him with the edge of the winnowing basket.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    This verse and later verses show how they had the ability to protect him out of affection. Fearing for the health of the child, Yaśodā and Rohiṇī could not do anything at first. Thus, when it mentions that they performed actions in the verse, they were not the major performers. The others had the two women with them in order to pacify their grief. They performed actions to protect the child everywhere (sarvataḥ or sarvaśaḥ), inside and outside, for auspiciousness. They did this in the best way (samyag). They waved a tassel made of cow tail hairs and as well waved mustard seeds and touched a winnowing basket.

    Personal Guidance
    Protecting Kṛṣṇa is protecting the cow — Gauḍīya household dharma weaves śaraṇāgati, go-rakṣya, and the vatsalya of elders into one shelter.
    Practical Applications
    • Today, perform one concrete act of go-rakṣya — buy milk from a protected-cow source, support a gośālā, or learn one traditional use of cow products you have forgotten.
    • Before any task involving a child or dependent, pause and silently entrust them to Kṛṣṇa the way Yaśodā and Rohiṇī did — protection is offered, never owned.
    • Refuse the modern conceit that 'the household' is a small or unspiritual sphere; treat your kitchen, your elders, and your cows as the very arena where Kṛṣṇa is sheltered.
    Analogy

    Yaśodā and Rohiṇī waving the cow-tail switch around baby Kṛṣṇa is the lighthouse-keeper, after the storm passes, still checking every lamp and rope before sleeping. Bhakti's protection of those entrusted to us does not stop when the visible danger is dead — it continues in small, embodied, lineage-given acts of go-rakṣya and mantra-cover.

    Stories
    Yudhiṣṭhira's care of his queens and mother

    After the Mahābhārata war, Yudhiṣṭhira (Anuśāsana-parva) personally oversaw protective rites and donations for Kuntī, Draupadī, and the women of the realm — not because the war was still on, but because dharmic households continue their guards even in peacetime.

    The Vraja matriarchs in 10.6.19 model the same instinct in domestic key: Pūtanā is dead, but the cow-tail still goes around the child, because true rakṣaṇa is a steady ritual atmosphere, not a single rescue.

    📝 My note

    Source: vedabase.io

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.20

    ↗
    गोमूत्रेण स्‍नापयित्वा पुनर्गोरजसार्भकम् । रक्षां चक्रुश्च शकृता द्वादशाङ्गेषु नामभि: ॥ २० ॥
    go-mūtreṇa snāpayitvā
    [go-mootrena snaapayitvaa]
    punar go-rajasārbhakam
    [punar go-rajasaarbhakam]
    rakṣāṁ cakruś ca śakṛtā
    [rakshaam chakrush cha shakritaa]

    [dvaadashaangeshu naamabhih]

    go - mūtreṇa — with the urine of the cows snāpayitvā — after thoroughly washing punaḥ — again go - rajasā — with the dust floating because of the movements of the cows arbhakam — unto the child rakṣām — protection cakruḥ — executed ca — also śakṛtā — with the cow dung dvādaśa - aṅgeṣu — in twelve places ( dvādaśa-tilaka ) nāmabhīḥ — by imprinting the holy names of the Lord .

    dvādaśa - aṅgeṣu
    in twelve places ( dvādaśa-tilaka )
    nāmabhīḥ
    by imprinting the holy names of the Lord .
    Important Words
    • go - mūtreṇa — with the urine of the cows
      Compound — go (cow) + mūtra (urine) + instrumental. A Vedic purificatory substance, treated as auspicious because the cow is offered to Kṛṣṇa. Gauḍīya practice retains go-substances as ritually pure, not from biology but from sambandha — the cow's relation to Govinda.
    • dvādaśa - aṅgeṣu — on twelve places of the body
      Compound — dvādaśa (twelve) + aṅga (limb) + locative plural. The Vaiṣṇava dvādaśa-tilaka — twelve namable points (forehead, throat, chest, navel, arms, etc.) marked with names of Viṣṇu. Still practiced daily in Gauḍīya saṁskṛti.
    • nāmabhīḥ — with the names (of the Lord)
      Instrumental plural of nāman. The marks are not random; each tilaka point gets a specific name (Keśava, Nārāyaṇa, Mādhava, etc.). Nāma is non-different from nāmī — to write a name on the body is to install the named.
    • go - rajasā — with the dust raised by the cows
      Compound — go (cow) + rajas (dust). The dust kicked up by cows returning from pasture is itself purifying. Vraja-rajas is celebrated in later cantos as the substance the Lord and His devotees roll in; the gopīs apply it to Him now.
    • rakṣām cakruḥ — executed protection
      Adjacent verb-phrase — rakṣām (protection, accusative) + cakruḥ (they made, perfect plural). The doing of the protection is the protection. Bhakti is verbal — devotional cover is not 'thought to' but 'done.'
    The child was thoroughly washed with cow urine and then smeared with the dust raised by the movements of the cows. Then different names of the Lord were applied with cow dung on twelve different parts of His body, beginning with the forehead, as done in applying tilaka. In this way, the child was given protection.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The child was thoroughly washed with cow urine and then smeared with the dust raised by the movements of the cows. Then different names of the Lord were applied with cow dung on twelve different parts of His body, beginning with the forehead, as done in applying tilaka. In this way, the child was given protection.
    Commentary

    Using cow dung, they protected Krsna by touching twelve places on his body while pronouncing twelve names of Visnu starting with Kesava on the forehead.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    This shows how they completely protected him. They protected him by touching cow dung to twelve parts of his body while chanting twelve names of Viṣṇu. The names are those mentioned in Padma Purāṇa when putting on tilaka. lalāṭe keśavaṁ dhyāyen nārāyaṇam athodare vakṣaḥ-sthale mādhavaṁ tu govindaṁ kaṇṭha-kūpake viṣṇuṁ ca dakṣiṇe kukṣau bāhau ca madhusūdanam trivikramaṁ kandhare tu vāmanaṁ vāma-pārśvake śrīdharaṁ vāma-bāhau tu hṛṣīkeśaṁ tu kandhare pṛṣṭhe ca padmanābhaṁ ca kaṭyāṁ dāmodaraṁ nyaset When one marks the forehead with tilaka, he must remember Keśava. When one marks the lower abdomen, he must remember Nārāyaṇa. For the chest, one should remember Mādhava, and when marking the hollow of the neck one should remember Govinda. Lord Viṣṇu should be remembered while marking the right side of the belly, and Madhusūdana should be remembered when marking the right arm. Trivikrama should be remembered when marking the right shoulder, and Vāmana should be remembered when marking the left side of the belly. Śrīdhara should be remembered while marking the left arm and Hṛṣīkeśa should be remembered when marking the left shoulder. Padmanābha and Dāmodara should be remembered when marking the back. Because they were the greatest Vaiṣṇavas they protected him by this method.

    • Radha Raman Sw SB 10-06-20-21 - 2016-09-01 Bhaktivedanta Manor
      His Holiness Radha Raman Swami· 2016-09-01· English
    Personal Guidance
    Sanctify the body with what Kṛṣṇa has touched — protection comes from marking every limb with His names, not from our own arrangements.
    Practical Applications
    • Mark the start of your day by audibly placing Kṛṣṇa's names over your body and work — forehead, hands, heart — before checking any device.
    • Refuse to lean on secular safeguards alone today; pair every practical precaution (lock, seatbelt, plan) with a brief invocation of a name of the Lord.
    • Honor what the devotees honor — treat cow-related substances, prasāda, and Vaiṣṇava remnants as carriers of protection, not superstition to be argued away.
    Analogy

    The gopīs bathing Kṛṣṇa with go-mūtra, marking Him with go-rajas and tilakas of cow-dung is the village priest writing the names of Hari on a child's body the way a banker stamps a sealed letter — the seal is unimpressive ink, the authority behind it is everything. The names, not the substances, do the work, and the cow is honored as the medium that carries them.

    Stories
    Pṛthu Mahārāja and the cow-earth

    When the Earth fled in cow-form before King Pṛthu (SB 4.18), she was finally persuaded to yield her wealth — milk, grains, and the means of human life — by being treated as mother and cow at once. Vedic civilization placed go-mātā at the foundation of human sustenance because go is dear to Govinda.

    The gopīs in 10.6.20, marking the body of the infant Kṛṣṇa with cow-derived substances, are at the most condensed point of that civilization: the very Govinda is being protected by the very gomātās He came to protect. The circuit is closed.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.21

    ↗
    गोप्य: संस्पृष्टसलिला अङ्गेषु करयो: पृथक् । न्यस्यात्मन्यथ बालस्य बीजन्यासमकुर्वत ॥ २१ ॥
    gopyaḥ saṁspṛṣṭa-salilā
    [gopyah samsprishta-salilaa]
    aṅgeṣu karayoḥ pṛthak
    [angeshu karayoh prithak]

    [nyasyaatmany atha baalasya]

    Blended reading (Sanskrit + English inline)

    Important Words
    • saṁspṛṣṭa - salilāḥ — having touched water (and sipped)
      Compound — saṁspṛṣṭa (touched) + salila (water). The ācamana act. Gauḍīya tradition holds that ācamana with viṣṇu-names purifies more than the hand; it aligns the prāṇas before any sevā.
    • nyasya — after placing (the letters)
      Gerund of ni-as (to place, deposit). The technical verb of mantra-nyāsa — placing syllables of a Viṣṇu-mantra on specific limbs (hand, heart, head). The body, marked with mantras, becomes a movable mandir.
    • bīja - nyāsam — the process of seed-mantra placement
      Compound — bīja (seed-syllable) + nyāsa (placement). Tantric vocabulary absorbed into Vaiṣṇava sevā. Bīja-mantras are the kernel-syllables of a deity-mantra; the Vraja mothers apply them to baby Kṛṣṇa with the same care the Gosvāmīs would later teach for Deity-worship.
    • ātmani — on their own (bodies)
      Locative of ātman — here used for the bodily self, not paramātman. The Gauḍīya reading carefully distinguishes the two: nyāsa is on the deha and indriyas of the gopīs, preparing them for sevā; ātmā in the higher sense is not modified, only purified in its instruments.
    • akurvata — (they) executed
      Imperfect middle plural of kṛ (to do). Middle voice — done for one's own benefit and the child's. Bhakti-vidhi is reflexive in this sense: the act on Kṛṣṇa is simultaneously the act on the devotee.
    The gopīs first executed the process of ācamana, drinking a sip of water from the right hand. They purified their bodies and hands with the nyāsa-mantra and then applied the same mantra upon the body of the child.
    Purport

    Nyāsa-mantra includes ācamana, or first drinking a sip of water kept in the right hand. There are different viṣṇu-mantras to purify the body. The gopīs, and in fact any householders, knew the process for being purified by chanting Vedic hymns. The gopīs executed this process first to purify themselves and then to purify the child Kṛṣṇa. One executes the process of aṅga-nyāsa and kara-nyāsa simply by drinking a little sip of water and chanting the mantra. The mantra is preceded with the first letter of the name, followed by anusvāra and the word namaḥ: oṁ namo ’jas tavāṅghrī avyāt, maṁ mano maṇimāṁs tava jānunī avyāt, and so on. By losing Indian culture, Indian householders have forgotten how to execute the aṅga-nyāsa and are simply busy in sense gratification, without any advanced knowledge of human civilization.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The gopīs first executed the process of ācamana, drinking a sip of water from the right hand. They purified their bodies and hands with the nyāsa-mantra and then applied the same mantra upon the body of the child.
    Commentary

    In the beginning, because of great fear, they began the rituals for protection without doing acamana. After calming down, they then performed the rituals in the proper way, by first performing acamana (samsprsta salila). They then performed anga nyasa and kara nyasa on their own bodies and hands. Then, on the child’s limbs such as feet, they invoked bijas. The bija consists of the first syllable of the name with an anusvara. Example: am namah, "May Aja protect your feet." Mam namah, "May Maniman protect your knees."

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Yaśodā and Rohiṇī are not included here as before. This is indicated by the word pṛthak (separately). The bīja-nyāsa is formed by the first syllable of the name of the Lord with an anusvara, followed by namaḥ (for instance yaṁ yajṣāya namaḥ). The names from aja to īśvara were used to touch eleven parts of Kṛṣṇa’s body.

    • Vrajendra Nandan Pr SB 10-06-21 Hindi - 2013-10-18 ISKCON Mira Road
      His Grace Vrajendra Nandan Prabhu· 2013-10-18· Hindi
    Personal Guidance
    Before approaching Kṛṣṇa or the child entrusted to you, purify body and hands with viṣṇu-mantra — sacred care begins with self-purification.
    Practical Applications
    • Before sitting for japa or study today, perform ācamana with attention, not as ritual habit but as a deliberate purification of the instruments you will offer to Kṛṣṇa.
    • Refuse to handle anything sacred — a Deity, a tulasī leaf, prasāda, a child in your care — until you have first cleaned your hands and quieted your mind with His name.
    • Notice where you have inherited 'Indian culture' as fashion but lost its purifying disciplines, and reclaim one small Vedic practice (ācamana, mantra before meals) starting tonight.
    Analogy

    The gopīs sipping water in ācamana, then placing mantra-syllables on their own hands before touching Kṛṣṇa, is the surgeon scrubbing in before lifting the newborn. The hands one offers to Bhagavān must be cleansed by Bhagavān's names first — sevā is sacred handling, and sacred handling begins with the handler.

    Stories
    Sanātana Gosvāmī teaching ārcana to the householder

    In the Hari-bhakti-vilāsa, Sanātana Gosvāmī systematized for Gauḍīyas exactly such daily details — ācamana, bīja-mantra-nyāsa, the placement of syllables on hands and limbs before approaching the Deity. He drew explicitly on Bhāgavata passages including this Pūtanā-rakṣā for authority.

    The gopīs in 10.6.21 are not performing folk-religion; they are doing what the Six Gosvāmīs would later codify for every Vaiṣṇava household. The chain of ārcana-vidhi reaches back to Vraja's mothers.

    Important Words
    • avyāt — may (the Lord) protect
      Optative of av (to protect). The opening verb of every line — a benediction, not a description. Each name of Bhagavān is grammatically asked to act; this is sevā as petition.
    • cakrī — the carrier of the disc
      From cakra (Sudarśana). The disc-bearing Viṣṇu protects in front because frontal attack is most direct; the disc itself is famously protective (recall Ambarīṣa). Gauḍīya note: cakra is named in the gopīs' prayer over the very person who wields it — līlā layered in līlā.
    • hariḥ — Lord Hari
      Nominative — 'the One who takes away.' Standard Bhāgavata name. In this kavaca, Hari guards the back — the direction most easily forgotten by the protected one.
    • haladharaḥ — Lord Haladhara
      Compound — hala (plow) + dhara (bearer). Balarāma, Kṛṣṇa's elder brother, the plow-wielder. The mantra ends with Haladhara because protection is finally familial, brotherly — Vraja's Kṛṣṇa is never without His Daujī behind Him.
    • samantāt — on all sides
      Indeclinable — closes the kavaca's geography. The protection has now covered every limb, every direction, every action. Nothing of Kṛṣṇa remains exposed — a 360-degree mantra-cocoon, modeled for every devotee household.
    [Śukadeva Gosvāmī informed Mahārāja Parīkṣit that the gopīs, following the proper system, protected Kṛṣṇa, their child, with this mantra.] May Aja protect Your legs, may Maṇimān protect Your knees, Yajña Your thighs, Acyuta the upper part of Your waist, and Hayagrīva Your abdomen. May Keśava protect Your heart, Īśa Your chest, the sun-god Your neck, Viṣṇu Your arms, Urukrama Your face, and Īśvara Your head. May Cakrī protect You from the front; may Śrī Hari, Gadādharī, the carrier of the club, protect You from the back; and may the carrier of the bow, who is known as the enemy of Madhu, and Lord Ajana, the carrier of the sword, protect Your two sides. May Lord Urugāya, the carrier of the conchshell, protect You from all corners; may Upendra protect You from above; may Garuḍa protect You on the ground; and may Lord Haladhara, the Supreme Person, protect You on all sides.
    Purport

    Even in the houses of the cultivators, who were not very advanced in the modern ways of civilization, the ladies used to know how to chant mantras to give protection to children with the help of cow dung and cow urine. This was a simple and practical way to give the greatest protection from the greatest dangers. People should know how to do this, for this is a part of Vedic civilization.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    [Śukadeva Gosvāmī informed Mahārāja Parīkṣit that the gopīs, following the proper system, protected Kṛṣṇa, their child, with this mantra.] May Aja protect Your legs, may Maṇimān protect Your knees, Yajṣa Your thighs, Acyuta the upper part of Your waist, and Hayagrīva Your abdomen. May Keśava protect Your heart, Īśa Your chest, the sun-god Your neck, Viṣṇu Your arms, Urukrama Your face, and Īśvara Your head. May Cakrī protect You from the front; may Śrī Hari, Gadādharī, the carrier of the club, protect You from the back; and may the carrier of the bow, who is known as the enemy of Madhu, and Lord Ajana, the carrier of the sword, protect Your two sides. May Lord Urugāya, the carrier of the conchshell, protect You from all corners; may Upendra protect You from above; may Garuḍa protect You on the ground; and may Lord Haladhara, the Supreme Person, protect You on all sides.
    Commentary

    They protected Krsna’s limbs using this verse. Anghri stands for the two feet (anghri). Maniman is a particular avatara of the Lord. Janu stands for both knees. Hrt refers to the lotus holding the jiva in the heart region. Uro means chest. In this verse the directions around Krsna are protected. May the holder of the cakra protect you in front. May Sahagada, the holder of the club, protect you behind. May Madhusudana holding the bow and aja holding a sword protect your sides. May Sankha urugaya, holding the conch, protect you in the four corners (north east, northwest, south east south west). May Upendra protect you above and Tarska (garuda) protect you below. May Haladhara protect you in all directions.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    May Cakrī protect you from the front; may Hari, the carrier of the club, protect you from the back; and may the carrier of the bow, who is known as the enemy of Madhu, protect one side and may Ajana, the carrier of the sword, protect your other side. May Urugāya, the carrier of the conchshell, protect you from all corners; may Upendra with Garuḍa protect you from above; may Haladhara protect you on the ground; and may Puruṣottama protect you on all sides. Tākrṣyaḥ should be grouped with Upendra. Puruṣaḥ means puruṣottamaḥ.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    May Cakrī protect you from the front; may Hari, the carrier of the club, protect you from the back; and may the carrier of the bow, who is known as the enemy of Madhu, protect one side and may Ajana, the carrier of the sword, protect your other side. May Urugāya, the carrier of the conchshell, protect you from all corners; may Upendra with Garuḍa protect you from above; may Haladhara protect you on the ground; and may Puruṣottama protect you on all sides. They call the names of the Lord like Ajita. Maṇimān is he who wears the Kaustubha jewel. Though all the names are suitable to protect all the limbs, and described in the Bhagavan-nāma-kaumudī, the particular forms should manifest quickly with their power since the forms are present in the names used as mantras. Or in Viṣṇu-dharmottara there is a prescription for chanting particular names for particular purposes. The heart is lower and chest is higher than that. Purusaḥ means purusottaḥ.

    Personal Guidance
    The gopīs invoke Kṛṣṇa's own forms to guard every limb of their child — true protection comes from placing one's body, head to toe, under His personal shelter.
    Practical Applications
    • Begin your morning by mentally offering each part of your body — feet, knees, heart, head — to Kṛṣṇa's protection before stepping into the day's work.
    • When sending a child or loved one out, pause and audibly invoke Kṛṣṇa's names over them rather than relying solely on locks, helmets, and apps.
    • Refuse the modern conceit that safety is engineered alone; treat the Vedic mantra-shelter as the first line of defense, not a quaint supplement.
    Analogy

    The gopīs invoking Aja, Maṇimān, Yajña, Acyuta, Hayagrīva, Keśava, Viṣṇu, Urukrama, Cakrī, Gadādharī, Madhuhā, Śaṅkhī, Upendra, Garuḍa, and Haladhara over each limb of Kṛṣṇa is the master jeweler setting one diamond after another into every facet of a single crown — every form of Bhagavān fits, because every form is His own. The body in their lap is the very source of these names, yet they crown Him with His own light so that none of His limbs goes uncovered.

    Stories
    The Nārāyaṇa-kavaca over Indra

    When Indra fled before Vṛtra and the demigods feared all was lost, Viśvarūpa gave Indra the Nārāyaṇa-kavaca (SB 6.8) — a limb-by-limb invocation of the Lord's many forms (Hari guard the head, Keśava the throat, Viṣṇu the heart, Acyuta the loins, etc.). Wearing it, Indra was untouchable.

    The rakṣā-mantra over baby Kṛṣṇa in 10.6.22–23 is the same kavaca-style — explicitly modeled on the Nārāyaṇa-kavaca structure. The gopīs are extending to the source Himself a defense that the demigods received from Him in earlier ages.

    Important Words
    • hṛṣīkeśaḥ — Lord Hṛṣīkeśa, master of the senses
      Compound — hṛṣīka (sense) + īśa (lord). The Gītā's own preferred name for Kṛṣṇa in His role as the rightful proprietor of the indriyas. Bhakti as hṛṣīkeṇa hṛṣīkeśa-sevanam (CC) — engaging the senses in their own owner's service.
    • nārāyaṇaḥ — Lord Nārāyaṇa
      Compound — nāra (water, beings) + ayana (resting place). The Lord as the abode of all jīvas, and especially as the protector of prāṇa (life-air). Vedic Vaiṣṇavism names Him at the very breath of the body, because life itself is His property.
    • śvetadvīpa - patiḥ — the master of Śvetadvīpa
      Compound — śveta (white) + dvīpa (island) + pati (lord). Bhāgavata locates Kṣīrodakaśāyī Viṣṇu on Śvetadvīpa, the white island of pure devotees. He is invoked to guard the citta (heart's core) because the heart is His personal indwelling-place as Paramātmā.
    • yogeśvaraḥ — Lord Yogeśvara, the master of yoga
      Compound — yoga + īśvara. The mind is yoga's battlefield (cf. Gītā 6.34); Yogeśvara is the only authority who can steady it. Gauḍīya reading: not the mind's own techniques, but surrender to Yogeśvara, is what finally fixes manas.
    • indriyāṇi — the senses
      Accusative plural of indriya. The senses are entrusted, not denied. The gopīs do not pray for the child's eyes and ears to be shut; they pray for them to be presided over by Hṛṣīkeśa.
    May Hṛṣīkeśa protect Your senses, and Nārāyaṇa Your life air. May the master of Śvetadvīpa protect the core of Your heart, and may Lord Yogeśvara protect Your mind.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    May Hṛṣīkeśa protect Your senses, and Nārāyaṇa Your life air. May the master of Śvetadvīpa protect the core of Your heart, and may Lord Yogeśvara protect Your mind.
    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The word avatu is repeated because of their anxiety to protect Kṛṣṇa. Similarly pātu is repeated in the next verse for the same reason.

    Personal Guidance
    Kṛṣṇa's many forms — Hṛṣīkeśa, Nārāyaṇa, Yogeśvara — guard the senses, prāṇa, heart, and mind; surrender each to Him by name today.
    Practical Applications
    • Before engaging the senses today, silently invoke Hṛṣīkeśa as their true master and offer their use back to Him.
    • When fear or agitation grips the heart, call on the Lord of Śvetadvīpa and refuse to let the mind run unguarded.
    • Begin the day naming each layer — senses, prāṇa, heart, mind — and place each under Kṛṣṇa's specific protection rather than relying on your own vigilance.
    Analogy

    Hṛṣīkeśa guarding the senses, Nārāyaṇa the prāṇas, Śvetadvīpa's Master the heart, Yogeśvara the mind is the four watchmen of a fort, each posted at one of the four gates that an enemy could enter — eye-gate, breath-gate, heart-gate, mind-gate. None of them is guessed at; each is named, and each gate has the right Lord standing in front of it.

    Stories
    Kapila's instruction to Devahūti

    Lord Kapila (SB 3.25–33) taught His mother Devahūti the systematic withdrawal of consciousness from senses → prāṇa → mind → buddhi → ātmā into Bhagavān. He named the inner gates one by one and gave them to the Lord's specific forms.

    The gopīs' invocation in 10.6.24 is the protective form of the same map: where Kapila walks His mother inward, the gopīs station the Lord's plenary forms at each level outward — defense and ascent share the same anatomy.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.25-26

    pṛśnigarbhas tu te buddhim

    Important Words
    • govindaḥ — Lord Govinda
      Compound — go (cow, senses, earth) + vinda (one who gives pleasure or recovers). The name first appears in Bhāgavata before the famous Govardhana līlā that will explain it (SB 10.27). Invoked here precisely for the krīḍā-state — playing — because Govinda is the Lord-of-play par excellence.
    • mādhavaḥ — Lord Mādhava
      From madhu (honey/sweetness, also the name of a slain demon) + va. Mādhava is the husband of Lakṣmī (Mā) and the source of sweetness; invoked over sleep because the mādhurya-rasa intensifies in restful, intimate hours.
    • vaikuṇṭhaḥ — Lord Vaikuṇṭha
      From vi (without) + kuṇṭha (obstruction) — 'the unobstructed one,' and by extension His abode. Invoked over walking, because the Lord whose movements never meet obstruction is the right guardian for our walking, where we so easily stumble.
    • yajñabhuk — Lord Yajñabhuk, the enjoyer of sacrifice
      Compound — yajña + bhuj (enjoyer). The Gītā's repeated declaration: bhoktāraṁ yajña-tapasāṁ (Gītā 5.29). He is invoked over eating, since for the devotee no food is private — every meal is yajña and He is its bhuktā.
    • sarva - graha - bhayam - karaḥ — (He who is) fear-producing to all malefic planets
      Compound — sarva (all) + graha (planet, seizer) + bhayam-kara (fear-causing). A startling phrase: the demigods of inauspiciousness themselves fear this Lord. Therefore He is the right guardian over the most exposed motion of the body — eating — when grahas are traditionally most active.
    May Lord Pṛśnigarbha protect Your intelligence, and the Supreme Personality of Godhead Your soul. While You are playing, may Govinda protect You, and while You are sleeping may Mādhava protect You. May Lord Vaikuṇṭha protect You while You are walking, and may Lord Nārāyaṇa, the husband of the goddess of fortune, protect You while You are sitting. Similarly, may Lord Yajñabhuk, the fearful enemy of all evil planets, always protect You while You enjoy life.
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    May Lord Pṛśnigarbha protect Your intelligence, and the Supreme Personality of Godhead Your soul. While You are playing, may Govinda protect You, and while You are sleeping may Mādhava protect You. May Lord Vaikuṇṭha protect You while You are walking, and may Lord Nārāyaṇa, the husband of the goddess of fortune, protect You while You are sitting. Similarly, may Lord Yajṣabhuk, the fearful enemy of all evil planets, always protect You while You enjoy life.
    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    Bhagavān paraḥ means Svayam Bhagavān. The subtle meaning here is that the expansions should protect Kṛṣṇa’s limbs since the expansions are the limbs in his possession and Svayam Bhagavān should protect himself (ātmānam) since he is the possessor of the limbs. The avatāras protecting his limbs are his āvaraṇa deities or protective forms surrounding him. Names are then mentioned in relation to Kṛṣṇa’s activities. Govinda means “he who plays among the cows.” The gopīs perceive different suitable forms in relation to the activities of Kṛṣṇa. Mādhava refers to the Lord sleeping on Śeṣa, while Lakṣmī massages his feet. He should protect Kṛṣṇa’s lying down and sleeping. Vaikuṇṭha is the avatāra who was the son of Vikuṇṭha, who came out of the palace using his feet to solve the quarrel between the Kumāras and Jaya and Vijaya. He should protect Kṛṣṇa’s walking. The husband of Lakṣmī refers to Mahā-nārāyaṇa who sits on a huge throne in Vaikuṇṭha. He should protect Kṛṣṇa’s sitting. The enjoyer of sacrifices should protect his enjoyment. The phrase “giving fear to all planets” should modify all the different forms of the Lord mentioned. All the deities mentioned protect Kṛṣṇa from the bad influence of planets while he performs all his activities.

    Śrīdhara Svāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The phrase “giving fear to all planets” should modify all the different forms of the Lord mentioned. All the deities mentioned protect Kṛṣṇa from the bad influence of planets while he performs all his activities.

    SB 10.6.22 SB 10.6.24 SB 10.6.27 BG 4.8 BG 1.1 BG 10.3 BG 9.11 BG 7.8 BG 6.35
    Personal Guidance
    Kṛṣṇa's plenary expansions guard every act — playing, sleeping, walking, sitting, eating — so entrust each ordinary motion of the day to Him.
    Practical Applications
    • Before each transition today — rising, walking out, sitting at work, eating, lying down — silently call on Kṛṣṇa by one of His names as Govinda, Mādhava, or Nārāyaṇa.
    • Refuse the illusion of self-protection: when fear of accident, illness, or unseen forces arises, remember that Yajñabhuk is the real shield and that anxious self-management is wasted ahaṅkāra.
    • Treat one ordinary meal today as offered food eaten under Yajñabhuk's protection — pause, offer it to Kṛṣṇa, and eat without distraction or grievance.
    Analogy

    Pṛśnigarbha for the intellect, Bhagavān for the soul, Govinda for playing, Mādhava for sleeping, Vaikuṇṭha for walking, Lakṣmī-pati for sitting, Yajñabhuk for eating is the wife who packs her child's lunchbox with a different little prayer for each break of the school day — one for the bell, one for the recess, one for the way home. Every motion the soul makes has a named Bhagavān assigned to it.

    Stories
    Mīrā singing through the day

    Mīrā Bai (Gauḍīya hagiography records her in the line of Vaiṣṇava bhaktās) is said to have sung to Giridhārī Gopāla through every movement of the day — grinding, drawing water, walking, eating, sleeping. She did not separate 'sādhana time' from 'kitchen time'; each motion was a name.

    The gopīs' rakṣā-mantra in 10.6.25–26 sanctifies exactly this attitude. Govinda when playing, Mādhava when sleeping — Mīrā lived this verse as adult bhakti.

    📝 My note

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.27-29

    ↗
    डाकिन्यो यातुधान्यश्च कुष्माण्डा येऽर्भकग्रहा: । भूतप्रेतपिशाचाश्च यक्षरक्षोविनायका: ॥ २७ ॥ कोटरा रेवती ज्येष्ठा पूतना मातृकादय: । उन्मादा ये ह्यपस्मारा देहप्राणेन्द्रियद्रुह: ॥ २८ ॥ स्वप्नद‍ृष्टा महोत्पाता वृद्धा बालग्रहाश्च ये । सर्वे नश्यन्तु ते विष्णोर्नामग्रहणभीरव: ॥ २९ ॥
    ḍākinyo yātudhānyaś ca
    [daakinyo yaatudhaanyash cha]
    kuṣmāṇḍā ye ’rbhaka-grahāḥ
    [kushmaandaa ye ’rbhaka-grahaah]
    bhūta-preta-piśācāś ca
    [bhoota-preta-pishaachaash cha]
    yakṣa-rakṣo-vināyakāḥ
    [yaksha-raksho-vinaayakaah]
    koṭarā revatī jyeṣṭhā
    [kotaraa revatee jyeshthaa]
    pūtanā mātṛkādayaḥ
    [pootanaa maatrikaadayah]
    unmādā ye hy apasmārā
    [unmaadaa ye hy apasmaaraa]
    deha-prāṇendriya-druhaḥ
    [deha-praanendriya-druhah]
    svapna-dṛṣṭā mahotpātā
    [svapna-drishtaa mahotpaataa]
    vṛddhā bāla-grahāś ca ye
    [vriddhaa baala-grahaash cha ye]
    sarve naśyantu te viṣṇor
    [sarve nashyantu te vishnor]
    nāma-grahaṇa-bhīravaḥ
    [naama-grahana-bheeravah]
    Word-for-word
    ḍākinyaḥ yātudhānyaḥ ca kuṣmāṇḍāḥ — witches and devils, enemies of children ye — those who are arbhaka - grahāḥ — like evil stars for children bhūta — evil spirits preta — evil hobgoblins piśācāḥ — similar bad spirits ca — also yakṣa — the living entities known as Yakṣas rakṣaḥ — those known as Rākṣasas vināyakāḥ — those by the name Vināyaka koṭarā — by the name Koṭarā revatī — by the name Revatī jyeṣṭhā — by the name Jyeṣṭhā pūtanā — by the name Pūtanā mātṛkā - ādayaḥ — and evil women like Mātṛkā unmādāḥ — those who cause madness ye — which others hi — indeed apasmārāḥ — causing loss of memory deha - prāṇa - indriya — to the body, life air and senses druhaḥ — give trouble svapna - dṛṣṭāḥ — the evil spirits that cause bad dreams mahā - utpātāḥ — those causing great disturbances vṛddhāḥ — the most experienced bāla - grahāḥ ca — and those attacking children ye — who sarve — all of them naśyantu — let be vanquished te — those viṣṇoḥ — of Lord Viṣṇu nāma - grahaṇa — by the chanting of the name bhīravaḥ — become afraid .

    Important Words
    • viṣṇoḥ — of Lord Viṣṇu
      Genitive — 'of Viṣṇu.' The Lord whose name is the weapon. Gauḍīya doctrine: nāma is non-different from nāmī; therefore viṣṇoḥ nāma is Viṣṇu personally acting in sound.
    • nāma - grahaṇa — the chanting / taking of the name
      Compound — nāma (name) + grahaṇa (taking, uttering). The technical phrase for harināma-kīrtana. Caitanya Mahāprabhu's whole mission rests on this verse-line: kṛṣṇa-nāma-grahaṇe sarva-pāpa nāśa — name-chanting destroys all demons and all sins.
    • bhīravaḥ — become afraid (lit. become afraid-ones)
      Nominative plural of bhīru — fearful ones. The very classes of beings that cause fear are themselves declared afraid at the Name. The grammar reverses the ordinary direction of bhaya: the terrifier is now the terrified.
    • deha - prāṇa - indriya — (troublers of) body, life-air, and senses
      Compound — deha + prāṇa + indriya. The Bhāgavata's standard tri-fold body-anatomy targeted by these forces. Each is also covered in 19–26 by a specific Lord — the verse's organization is consistent.
    • arbhaka - grahāḥ — (evil influences) attacking children
      Compound — arbhaka (child) + graha (seizer). The Pūtanā-class. Bhāgavata names them not to dignify them but to dismiss them en bloc by the Name. Vaiṣṇava pastoral counsel: parents are taught to invoke viṣṇu-nāma over children precisely because these classes are real.
    The evil witches known as Ḍākinīs, Yātudhānīs and Kuṣmāṇḍas are the greatest enemies of children, and the evil spirits like Bhūtas, Pretas, Piśācas, Yakṣas, Rākṣasas and Vināyakas, as well as witches like Koṭarā, Revatī, Jyeṣṭhā, Pūtanā and Mātṛkā, are always ready to give trouble to the body, the life air and the senses, causing loss of memory, madness and bad dreams. Like the most experienced evil stars, they all create great disturbances, especially for children, but one can vanquish them simply by uttering Lord Viṣṇu’s name, for when Lord Viṣṇu’s name resounds, all of them become afraid and go away.
    Purport

    As stated in the Brahma-saṁhitā (5.33) : advaitam acyutam anādim ananta-rūpam ādyaṁ purāṇa-puruṣaṁ nava-yauvanaṁ ca vedeṣu durlabham adurlabham ātma-bhaktau govindam ādi-puruṣaṁ tam ahaṁ bhajāmi “I worship the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Govinda, who is the original person — nondual, infallible, and without beginning. Although He expands into unlimited forms, He is still the original, and although He is the oldest person, He always appears as a fresh youth. Such eternal, blissful and all-knowing forms of the Lord cannot be understood by the academic wisdom of the Vedas, but they are always manifest to pure, unalloyed devotees.” While decorating the body with tilaka, we give protection to the body by chanting twelve names of Viṣṇu. Although Govinda, or Lord Viṣṇu, is one, He has different names and forms with which to act differently. But if one cannot remember all the names at one time, one may simply chant, “Lord Viṣṇu, Lord Viṣṇu, Lord Viṣṇu,” and always think of Lord Viṣṇu. Viṣṇor ārādhanaṁ param: this is the highest form of worship. If one remembers Viṣṇu always, even though one is disturbed by many bad elements, one can be protected without a doubt. The Āyurveda-śāstra recommends, auṣadhi cintayet viṣṇum: even while taking medicine, one should remember Viṣṇu, because the medicine is not all and all and Lord Viṣṇu is the real protector. The material world is full of danger ( padaṁ padaṁ yad vipadām ). Therefore one must become a Vaiṣṇava and think of Viṣṇu constantly. This is made easier by the chanting of the Hare Kṛṣṇa mahā-mantra. Therefore Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu has recommended, kīrtanīyaḥ sadā hariḥ paraṁ vijayate śrī-kṛṣṇa-saṅkīrtanam, and kīrtanād eva kṛṣṇasya mukta-saṅgaḥ paraṁ vrajet.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    The evil witches known as Ḍākinīs, Yātudhānīs and Kuṣmāṇḍas are the greatest enemies of children, and the evil spirits like Bhūtas, Pretas, Piśācas, Yakṣas, Rākṣasas and Vināyakas, as well as witches like Koṭarā, Revatī, Jyeṣṭhā, Pūtanā and Mātṛkā, are always ready to give trouble to the body, the life air and the senses, causing loss of memory, madness and bad dreams. Like the most experienced evil stars, they all create great disturbances, especially for children, but one can vanquish them simply by uttering Lord Viṣṇu's name, for when Lord Viṣṇu's name resounds, all of them become afraid and go away.
    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    This list should also include Carakīs and Jvālāmukhīs. Bāla-grahāḥ is repeated (arbhaka-grahāḥ) because children have intense fear of them. Some say that arbhaka-grahas are those who snatch living beings by taking the form of a child. These are a type of Piśāca.

    Related values & verses

    • Suresvar Pr SB 10-06-27-29 Hindi Translation - 2013-10-20 ISKCON Mira Road
      His Grace Suresvar Prabhu· 2013-10-20· Hindi
    Personal Guidance
    In a world full of unseen disturbances, chant Lord Viṣṇu's names — His holy name itself is the protection no medicine or precaution can replace.
    Practical Applications
    • Begin the day by audibly chanting 'Viṣṇu, Viṣṇu, Viṣṇu' or the Hare Kṛṣṇa mahā-mantra before reaching for your phone, planting His name as the first sound of your day.
    • When anxiety, bad dreams, or intrusive thoughts arise, refuse to fight them mentally — instead chant Kṛṣṇa's name aloud and let the disturbance retreat on its own.
    • While taking medicine, food, or any precaution today, remember auṣadhi cintayet viṣṇum — silently offer it to Viṣṇu, acknowledging Him as the real protector behind every remedy.
    Analogy

    Listing Ḍākinīs, Yātudhānīs, Kuṣmāṇḍas, Bhūtas, Pretas, Piśācas, Yakṣas, Rākṣasas, Vināyakas, Koṭarā, Revatī, Jyeṣṭhā, Pūtanā, Mātṛkās — and then declaring that Lord Viṣṇu's name vanquishes all of them — is the village notary reading out the long catalogue of debts before tearing the page in half. The list is exhaustive on purpose, so that no debt-collector can later say, 'But my kind was not named.' The Name covers all of them at once.

    Stories
    Ajāmila's single Nārāyaṇa

    The fallen Ajāmila (SB 6.1–3), at the hour of death, called the name of his son Nārāyaṇa — and the Viṣṇudūtas appeared, scattering the Yamadūtas. The Yamadūtas' detailed list of his sins, like the Bhāgavata's list of grahas in 10.6.27–29, was real and accurate. The Name still trumped it.

    The Bhāgavata in this chapter, in two registers — protective rakṣā for the infant Kṛṣṇa and pratimukti for the dying brāhmaṇa — teaches the same single fact: viṣṇoḥ nāma-grahaṇam empties every demon's catalogue.

    Haridāsa Ṭhākura before the maṅkārī

    Gauḍīya hagiography (Caitanya-caritāmṛta Antya-līlā 3) records that when a beautiful prostitute was sent by an enemy to seduce Haridāsa Ṭhākura, she sat before him for three nights as he continued his three-lakh-name daily japa. The mātṛkā-form of māyā in feminine guise dissolved against the harināma; she ended as his disciple.

    Verses 27–29 are the doctrinal basis for that pastime: viṣṇoḥ nāma-grahaṇa-bhīravaḥ — every disturbing form is afraid at the chanting of the Name.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.30

    ↗
    श्रीशुक उवाच इति प्रणयबद्धाभिर्गोपीभि: कृतरक्षणम् । पाययित्वा स्तनं माता सन्न्यवेशयदात्मजम् ॥ ३० ॥
    śrī-śuka uvāca
    [shree-shuka uvaacha]
    iti praṇaya-baddhābhir
    [iti pranaya-baddhaabhir]
    gopībhiḥ kṛta-rakṣaṇam
    [gopeebhih krita-rakshanam]
    pāyayitvā stanaṁ mātā
    [paayayitvaa stanam maataa]
    sannyaveśayad ātmajam
    [sannyaveshayad aatmajam]
    Word-for-word
    śrī - śukaḥ uvāca — Śrī Śukadeva Gosvāmī said iti — in this way praṇaya - baddhābhiḥ — who were bound with maternal affection gopībhiḥ — by the elderly gopīs, headed by mother Yaśodā kṛta - rakṣaṇam — all measures were taken to protect the child pāyayitvā — and after that, feeding the child stanam — the nipple mātā — mother Yaśodā sannyaveśayat — made to lie down on the bed ātmajam — her son .

    Important Words
    • praṇaya - baddhābhiḥ — (by the gopīs) who were bound by maternal affection
      Compound — praṇaya (deep affection, intimacy) + baddha (bound) + feminine plural instrumental. Praṇaya is a higher emotional grade than mere sneha — in Rūpa Gosvāmī's Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu, praṇaya is a sub-stage in the maturation of prema. The gopīs' bond is already at that level.
    • kṛta - rakṣaṇam — (unto whom) protection had been performed
      Compound — kṛta (done) + rakṣaṇa (protection). The rakṣā-mantras of verses 19–29 are summed up in this single past-participial phrase. Vedic and Vaiṣṇava ritual: name the act, then close it; only then return to ordinary care.
    • pāyayitvā — after feeding (the breast)
      Causative gerund of pā (to drink). She caused Him to drink — gentle, ordinary, repeated. After the high-key kavaca, the Bhāgavata steps back into vātsalya's plainest verb.
    • sannyaveśayat — made (Him) lie down (on the bed)
      Causative imperfect of sam-ni-viś (to settle). The same verb-family used for ritual installation of a Deity in His temple bed. Yaśodā is, in effect, performing nidrā-sevā for Bhagavān; she does not call it that, which is why it is so dear to Him.
    • ātmajam — her son
      Compound — ātma (self) + ja (born from). 'Born from one's own self.' Gauḍīya note: in Yaśodā's vātsalya the bhāva of 'my own child' is given by Yoga-māyā even though Kṛṣṇa is not biologically hers — the bhāva is doctrinally true at the level of love, not biology. Bhagavān accepts the relationship as real.
    Śrīla Śukadeva Gosvāmī continued: All the gopīs, headed by mother Yaśodā, were bound by maternal affection. After they thus chanted mantras to protect the child, mother Yaśodā gave the child the nipple of her breast to suck and then got Him to lie down on His bed.
    Purport

    When a baby drinks milk from the breast of his mother, this is a good sign of health. So the elderly gopīs were not satisfied with chanting mantras to give protection to Kṛṣṇa; they also tested whether their child’s health was in order. When the child sucked the breast, this confirmed that He was healthy, and when the gopīs were fully satisfied, they had the child lie down on His bed.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Śrīla Śukadeva Gosvāmī continued: All the gopīs, headed by mother Yaśodā, were bound by maternal affection. After they thus chanted mantras to protect the child, mother Yaśodā gave the child the nipple of her breast to suck and then got Him to lie down on His bed.
    Commentary

    Thus the gopis completed the rituals for protection, bound to Yasoda’s house by affection. Yasoda then made Krsna drink milk from her breast. By his drinking she could understand that he was in good health. Then she put him to sleep.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The gopīs protected Kṛṣṇa because of being bound by great affection. They always remained in Yaśodā’s house as nurses because of that affection. They engaged in various activities there because of great eagerness for the Lord. Yaśodā showed her natural affection as a mother by giving her breast. This is also a customary way of seeing if the child is in good health.

    Personal Guidance
    Yaśodā's anxious tending of baby Kṛṣṇa shows that bhakti expresses itself as concrete, embodied care — love verified by attention, not sentiment.
    Practical Applications
    • Tend to one dependent — child, elder, or junior — today with the same patient, hands-on attentiveness Yaśodā gave Kṛṣṇa, not delegated and not rushed.
    • Refuse the false choice between 'spiritual' acts and bodily care; offer the next ordinary nurturing task (feeding, settling, comforting) directly to Kṛṣṇa as sevā.
    • Notice where you stop at chanting or wishing and skip the practical follow-through, and finish at least one such task today before lying down to rest.
    Analogy

    Yaśodā, having performed the rakṣā-mantra, now lays the child to her breast and to His bed — exactly as a master architect, after blessing the foundations, returns to washing the dishes. Pure vātsalya is small, near, and embodied; it does not float above its child in mystic abstraction but settles, nipple-to-mouth, into ordinary care.

    Stories
    Kausalyā tending the boy Rāma

    In Ayodhyā-kāṇḍa, the Rāmāyaṇa lingers on Kausalyā's daily acts of motherly care for Rāma — bathing, feeding, plaiting hair — before His exile to the forest. She is queen-consort of Daśaratha; her hands still hold milk and oils.

    Yaśodā in 10.6.30, having just protected her child with high mantras, does precisely the same — feeds, lays Him down, smooths the cloth. Gauḍīya rasa-vicāra reads Yaśodā's vātsalya as higher than Kausalyā's; the verse models its texture: post-mantra, the milk.

    struck with wonder .
    Important Words
    • vismitāḥ — struck with wonder
      Nominative plural past participle of vi-smi (to be amazed). Bhāgavata's signature emotion at the līlā: vismaya, the speechless astonishment that precedes faith. Gauḍīya rasa-vicāra lists adbhuta-rasa as a vyabhicāri-bhāva supporting all primary rasas — wonder underwrites every loving response.
    • pūtanā - deham — the (gigantic) body of Pūtanā
      Compound — Pūtanā + deha (body) + accusative. The leftover of māyā after Kṛṣṇa has acted. Bhāgavata is unsentimental: His mercy leaves visible wreckage, and the devotee does not pretend the wreckage is not there.
    • nanda - ādayaḥ — headed by Nanda Mahārāja
      Compound — Nanda + ādi (etc.). Nanda is named first because as gopa-rāja he is the responsible elder. The verse maintains the social order even at the moment of cosmic wonder.
    • mathurāyāḥ — from Mathurā
      Ablative of Mathurā. The trip was business — taxes, perhaps offerings — at Kaṁsa's capital. Bhāgavata-tattva note: ordinary commerce was running in Mathurā while in Vraja the Lord was slaying His first demon. Kṛṣṇa's līlā moves on a separate timeline from the world's news cycle.
    • vrajam — to Vṛndāvana / Vraja
      Accusative — to Vraja. The verse's geography is precise: Mathurā outward, Vraja inward. Gauḍīya tīrtha-vicāra makes much of this — Mathurā is great, but Vraja (Goloka's manifestation) is greater.
    Meanwhile, all the cowherd men, headed by Nanda Mahārāja, returned from Mathurā, and when they saw on the way the gigantic body of Pūtanā lying dead, they were struck with great wonder.
    Purport

    Nanda Mahārāja’s wonder may be understood in various ways. First of all, the cowherd men had never before seen such a gigantic body in Vṛndāvana, and therefore they were struck with wonder. Then they began to consider where such a body had come from, whether it had dropped from the sky, or whether, by some mistake or by the power of some mystic yoginī, they had come to some place other than Vṛndāvana. They could not actually guess what had happened, and therefore they were struck with wonder.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Meanwhile, all the cowherd men, headed by Nanda Mahārāja, returned from Mathurā, and when they saw on the way the gigantic body of Pūtanā lying dead, they were struck with great wonder.
    Commentary

    The cowherd men were astonished. Perhaps by mistake, a mountain with unclipped wings fell from the sky and crushed all the huge trees. Perhaps, under the spell of some witch we have come to the wrong place. Or perhaps this is just a magical illusion. In this way many doubts arose in their minds.

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    The cowherds returned quickly to Vraja. That was mentioned. Coming from Mathurā they came within sight of Vraja. From afar they thought Pūtanā might be a mountain. They imagined various things since at a distance they could see the body properly. They were astonished because Vasudeva’s words about disturbance had come true.

    Related values & verses

    • Revati Raman Pr SB 10-06-31 - 2009-05-20 ISKCON Tirupati
      His Grace Revati Raman Prabhu· 2009-05-20· Telugu
    • BPSPSw SB 10-06-31 Hindi - 2013-10-22 ISKCON Mira Road
      His Holiness Bhakti Padma Saurabh Pracharak Swami· 2013-10-22· Hindi
    Personal Guidance
    When Kṛṣṇa silently destroys a Pūtanā in your life, you may return to find only the wreckage — wonder, then trace it back to His mercy.
    Practical Applications
    • Notice one disaster Kṛṣṇa quietly averted while you were elsewhere today, and name it aloud as His protection rather than your luck.
    • When you encounter the 'gigantic corpse' of a problem already resolved without you, refuse the impulse to credit chance — sit with the wonder and offer thanks.
    • Speak to one family member tonight about a danger Kṛṣṇa removed from your household, so the wonder of Nanda Mahārāja becomes shared kṛtajñatā, not private sentiment.
    Analogy

    Nanda's party returning from Mathurā to find the twelve-mile corpse of Pūtanā lying across the pasture is the householder who comes home from a business trip to find a great oak felled in his garden — he did not chop it, no storm was reported, and the trunk still steams. So we sometimes return to our lives and discover that Kṛṣṇa, while we were elsewhere, has quietly removed what we did not even know was there.

    Stories
    Yudhiṣṭhira returning to find Aśvatthāmā stopped

    When Aśvatthāmā unleashed the brahmāstra against the womb of Uttarā, Kṛṣṇa personally entered to protect the embryo Parīkṣit (SB 1.7–8, 1.12). The Pāṇḍavas, who had been managing the larger war's aftermath, learned only later what had been averted in their absence.

    Nanda's wonder in 10.6.31 is the householder version of the Pāṇḍava arc: come back from your Mathurā-trip, count the disasters Kṛṣṇa quietly disarmed while you were busy, and let the wonder become kṛtajñatā, not luck.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.32

    ↗
    नूनं बतर्षि: सञ्जातो योगेशो वा समास स: । स एव द‍ृष्टो ह्युत्पातो यदाहानकदुन्दुभि: ॥ ३२ ॥
    nūnaṁ batarṣiḥ sañjāto

    ānakadundubhiḥ
    Ānakadundubhi (another name of Vasudeva) .
    Important Words
    • ṛṣiḥ — a great saintly person
      Nominative — a Vedic seer. Bhāgavata uses ṛṣi here in its functional sense: one whose attention is so trained on truth that foresight follows. Gauḍīya note: a Vaiṣṇava becomes ṛṣi not by mantra-siddhi but by anugraha — Kṛṣṇa's grace clarifying his sight.
    • yoga - īśaḥ — a master of mystic power
      Compound — yoga + īśa. Lord of yogic potency. Nanda's mistake is touching: he assumes Vasudeva must have aṣṭa-siddhis to foresee danger. The Bhāgavata corrects this gently across the canto — true Vaiṣṇavas are yogeśas only in the sense that they have Yogeśvara as their Lord.
    • utpātaḥ — kind of disturbance
      Nominative of utpāta — an inauspicious portent. Bhāgavata vocabulary for cosmic disturbance (echoing verse 1's utpāta-āgama-śaṅkitaḥ). The episode closes the loop opened in verse 1: the utpāta Nanda feared has been described, and now he names it.
    • āha — (he) said / predicted
      Perfect of brū (defective root, perfect āha = 'he said'). Vasudeva spoke; that simple act of speech turned out to be foresight. Bhāgavata's sober register: prophecy in this tradition usually looks like a careful sentence.
    • ānakadundubhiḥ — Ānakadundubhi (Vasudeva)
      Compound — ānaka (kettle-drum) + dundubhi (large drum). Vasudeva's epithet — at his birth the drums of the demigods sounded. Bhāgavata names him by this title here precisely to underline his cosmic stature, which Nanda has only just begun to perceive.
    Nanda Mahārāja and the other gopas exclaimed: My dear friends, you must know that Ānakadundubhi, Vasudeva, has become a great saint or a master of mystic power. Otherwise how could he have foreseen this calamity and predicted it to us?
    Purport

    This verse illustrates the difference between kṣatriyas and innocent vaiśyas. By studying the political situation, Vasudeva could see what would happen, whereas Nanda Mahārāja, the king of the agriculturalists, could only guess that Vasudeva was a great saintly person and had developed mystic powers. Vasudeva actually had all mystic powers under his control; otherwise he could not have become the father of Kṛṣṇa. But in fact he foresaw the calamities in Vraja by studying Kaṁsa’s political activities and thus warned Nanda Mahārāja to take precautions, although Nanda Mahārāja thought that Vasudeva had predicted this incident through wonderful mystic powers. By mystic powers gained through the practice of haṭha-yoga, one can study and understand the future.

    — A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupāda
    Gauḍīya Ācāryas
    Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura Gauḍīya
    Translation
    Nanda Mahārāja and the other gopas exclaimed: My dear friends, you must know that Ānakadundubhi, Vasudeva, has become a great saint or a master of mystic power. Otherwise how could he have foreseen this calamity and predicted it to us?
    Commentary

    "Certainly (nunam) Vasudeva is a rsi in our family; being all knowing, he has predicted correctly. Through practice of astanga yoga (yogesa) he has developed the eyes to see the future. He shines preminently (sam asa)."

    Jīva Gosvāmī Gauḍīya
    Commentary

    They talked among themselves in astonishment. Nūnam indicates conjecture. Vasudeva was not like this before. Now he has become a real sage because he makes authoritative statements like the sages. “But we cannot see the symptoms of a sage in him.” Then perhaps he is a master of yoga—fixed on internal worship. The words saṣjātaḥ and samāsa both have the affix sam. They indicate “being fixed.” What is the reason for saying this? He saw these disturbances. He is Anakadundubhi. The drums announced his qualification for this at his birth. Or, in a previous birth perhaps he was a sage or yogī. Now he has been born as Vasudeva. Because he is omniscient he spoke in this manner. This is how they thought. Thus they were astonished.

    Personal Guidance
    Vasudeva's foresight came from sober study, not mystic display — clear seeing in Kṛṣṇa's service is itself the saint's true power.
    Practical Applications
    • Study one concrete situation in your life today — at work, at home — the way Vasudeva studied Kaṁsa, and warn whoever needs warning before the calamity lands.
    • Refuse to mistake genuine devotional discernment for mystical theatrics; honor the quiet, sober vision of those serving Kṛṣṇa around you.
    • Notice when you, like Nanda Mahārāja, attribute another's clear thinking to magic — and instead ask what sober attention they paid that you skipped.
    Analogy

    Nanda's praise of Vasudeva — 'Surely he has become a ṛṣi or a yogeśvara, how else could he have foreseen this?' — is the villager praising the calm neighbor who, weeks before the flood, had quietly raised his floor by a foot. Vasudeva did no magic; he paid attention. The Bhāgavata's whole vision of the sober devotee is folded into Nanda's reverence here.

    Stories
    Vidura's quiet foresight at Hastināpura

    All through the Mahābhārata's lead-up to war, Vidura — Kṛṣṇa-bhakta in the Kuru court — quietly warned Yudhiṣṭhira, Kuntī, and Dhṛtarāṣṭra of what was coming (Mahābhārata, Udyoga-parva). He did not perform yogic flights or mystic displays; he simply read dharma carefully and spoke when others were silent.

    Nanda's praise of Vasudeva in 10.6.32 catches exactly this kind of saint. The devotee who 'foresees' is usually just the devotee who has been awake all along while the rest of us were asleep — and Bhāgavata calls that wakefulness ṛṣitva.

    Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam 10.6.33

    ↗
    कलेवरं परशुभिश्छित्त्वा तत्ते व्रजौकस: । दूरे क्षिप्‍त्वावयवशो न्यदहन् काष्ठवेष्टितम् ॥ ३३ ॥
    kalevaraṁ paraśubhiś
    [kalevaram parashubhish]

    [chittvaa tat te vrajaukasah]
